Тема: № 21 Описание обязанностей и должностей (Job Duties and Responsibilities)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. duties [ˈdjuːtiz] — обязанности
2. to manage [ˈmænɪdʒ] — управлять, руководить
3. to coordinate [kəʊˈɔːdɪneɪt] — координировать
4. to be in charge of [biː ɪn tʃɑːdʒ ɒv] — отвечать за, быть ответственным за
5. to handle [ˈhændl] — обрабатывать, справляться с
6. to report to [rɪˈpɔːt tuː] — подчиняться, отчитываться перед

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~440 слов.
"Welcome to the team, Jessica!" said Mark, the department head. "I'm glad you're here. Let me explain your duties and responsibilities."

Jessica sat down with a notebook. "Great, I'm ready to learn."

"First, you'll be in charge of the social media accounts," Mark explained. "You'll handle content creation, scheduling posts, and responding to comments."

"Okay, so I'm responsible for all social media platforms?" Jessica asked.

"Yes. You'll also coordinate with the design team to create visuals for our campaigns. You'll report to me directly, and we'll have weekly meetings to review your progress."

Jessica wrote everything down. "What about analytics? Will I track the performance of our posts?"

"Absolutely," Mark said. "You'll prepare monthly reports showing engagement rates, follower growth, and campaign results. You'll present these reports to the marketing director."

"That sounds like a lot of responsibility," Jessica said with a smile.

"It is," Mark admitted. "But I think you're ready for it. You have great experience from your previous job. By the way, you'll also manage our intern, Alex. He'll help you with content scheduling and basic analytics."

"Perfect," Jessica nodded. "I enjoy mentoring people."

"One more thing," Mark added. "Sometimes we have urgent campaigns that need quick turnaround. In those cases, you'll need to handle multiple tasks at once and prioritize your work."

"No problem," Jessica said. "I'm good at multitasking."

Mark smiled. "Great. Do you have any questions about your duties?"

"Just one," Jessica said. "What are the main goals for this position in the first three months?"

"Good question," Mark said. "First, I want you to increase our Instagram followers by 20%. Second, improve our engagement rate by 15%. And third, launch at least two successful campaigns."

Jessica felt excited. "I'll get started right away."

For the first few weeks, Jessica was very busy. She created content calendars, met with the design team, trained Alex, and analyzed data every day. Sometimes she felt overwhelmed, but she remembered Mark's advice about prioritizing.

After three months, Jessica prepared her first big presentation. She showed the marketing director all the results: Instagram followers increased by 25%, engagement rate went up by 18%, and both campaigns were very successful.

The director was impressed. "Excellent work, Jessica. You've exceeded our expectations."

Jessica smiled. She realized that understanding your duties and responsibilities is the first step to success. When you know exactly what you need to do, you can focus your energy and achieve great results.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What was Jessica in charge of?
2. Who did Jessica report to?
3. What were the main goals for Jessica in the first three months?

Тема: № 22 Карьерный рост и развитие (Career Growth and Development)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. promotion [prəˈməʊʃn] — повышение
2. to develop [dɪˈveləp] — развивать
3. to set goals [set ɡəʊlz] — ставить цели
4. to learn new skills [lɜːn njuː skɪlz] — изучать новые навыки
5. to take initiative [teɪk ɪˈnɪʃətɪv] — проявлять инициативу
6. to achieve [əˈtʃiːv] — достигать

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~450 слов.
"Hey, Mike! Congratulations on your promotion!" said his colleague, Anna. "Senior Project Manager! That's amazing!"

Mike smiled. "Thanks, Anna. I'm really excited about it."

"I bet," Anna said. "You've been working here for three years now. How did you do it? What's your secret?"

Mike thought for a moment. "I don't think there's a secret. I just set clear goals and worked hard to achieve them."

"Can you give me some advice?" Anna asked. "I want to grow in my career too."

"Sure," Mike said. "First, you need to learn new skills all the time. The industry is changing fast, and you can't stay in the same place. I took online courses in project management and leadership."

"That's a good idea," Anna nodded. "What else?"

"Second, take initiative. Don't just wait for someone to give you tasks. If you see a problem, try to solve it. If you have an idea, share it with your manager."

Anna wrote it down. "I'm usually shy about speaking up in meetings."

"You need to get over that," Mike said gently. "Your ideas are valuable. When I started, I was quiet too. But I forced myself to speak up, and people noticed."

"What about setting goals?" Anna asked.

"That's crucial," Mike said. "I sat down with my manager every six months and discussed my career path. I said, 'I want to become a Senior Project Manager in two years. What do I need to do to get there?' My manager gave me specific tasks and projects to work on."

"Did you always meet your goals?" Anna asked.

"Not always," Mike admitted. "Sometimes I failed, but I learned from my mistakes. The important thing is to keep moving forward."

Anna thought about this. "So it's about continuous learning, taking initiative, and setting clear goals?"

"Exactly," Mike said. "And don't forget about networking. Build relationships with people in your company and in your industry. Sometimes opportunities come from connections."

"Thanks for the advice, Mike," Anna said. "I'm going to start working on my career development plan today."

"Good luck!" Mike said. "Remember, career growth doesn't happen overnight. It takes time, effort, and patience. But if you stay committed, you'll achieve your goals."

Six months later, Anna got her own promotion. She became a Team Lead. She called Mike to thank him.

"I couldn't have done it without your advice," she said. "I took those courses, started speaking up in meetings, and set clear goals with my manager."

"I'm proud of you," Mike said. "You proved that anyone can grow in their career if they're willing to put in the work."

Anna smiled. She realized that career development is not about luck or connections. It's about taking responsibility for your own growth and never stopping learning.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):

2. What advice did Mike give Anna about speaking up?
3. How often did Mike discuss his career path with his manager?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):

He also emphasized the importance of... (networking).

Тема: № 23 Навыки и квалификации (Skills and Qualifications)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. skill [skɪl] — навык
2. qualification [ˌkwɒlɪfɪˈkeɪʃn] — квалификация, образование
3. experience [ɪkˈspɪəriəns] — опыт
4. to improve [ɪmˈpruːv] — улучшать
5. certificate [səˈtɪfɪkət] — сертификат
6. to be proficient in [biː prəˈfɪʃnt ɪn] — владеть в совершенстве

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~440 слов.
"So, David, I heard you're thinking about changing jobs," said Lisa during their lunch break.

David nodded. "Yeah, I want to move to a bigger company. But I'm not sure if I have the right skills and qualifications."

Lisa leaned forward. "What do you mean? You've been working in marketing for five years!"

"I know, but the job descriptions always ask for specific things like digital marketing certificates, advanced Excel skills, or project management qualifications. I feel like I'm missing something."

Lisa smiled. "Let me help you. First, let's look at your current skills. What are you good at?"

David thought for a moment. "Well, I'm proficient in social media marketing. I can create content, manage campaigns, and analyze data. I also have experience in team leadership."

"That's great!" Lisa said. "But you need to show it on your resume. Don't just write 'social media marketing.' Write specific achievements like 'increased Instagram followers by 40% in six months.'"

"Good point," David said, taking notes. "What about qualifications? I have a bachelor's degree in business, but is that enough?"

"For most marketing positions, yes," Lisa explained. "But if you want to stand out, consider getting additional certificates. There are online courses in Google Analytics, Facebook Ads, or even Agile project management. They're not expensive and you can study at your own pace."

David looked interested. "How long does it take to get these certificates?"

"Usually a few weeks to a couple of months. And they look great on your resume. Employers love to see that you're continuously improving your skills."

"What about soft skills?" David asked. "Like communication or problem-solving?"

"Those are crucial too!" Lisa said. "But they're harder to prove. The best way is to give examples in your interview. Like, 'I used my communication skills to resolve a conflict between two team members' or 'I improved our workflow by solving a scheduling problem.'"

David smiled. "This is really helpful. I feel more confident now."

"Good!" Lisa said. "Start by updating your resume with your current skills. Then, choose one or two certificates to get in the next three months. And practice talking about your experience in a way that shows your value."

"Thanks, Lisa. I'll start tonight," David said.

Two months later, David got a new job at a major tech company. He called Lisa to thank her.

"I couldn't have done it without your advice," he said. "I got my Google Analytics certificate and rewrote my resume. The interviewer was really impressed with my specific achievements."

"I'm so happy for you!" Lisa said. "See? You had all the skills and qualifications you needed. You just had to present them properly."

David realized that knowing your skills and continuously improving them is the key to career success.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)

Тема: № 24 Офисное оборудование и техника (Office Equipment and Technology)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. printer [ˈprɪntə] — принтер
2. to scan [skæn] — сканировать
3. to break down [breɪk daʊn] — ломаться
4. IT support [ˌaɪ ˈtiː səˈpɔːt] — техническая поддержка
5. to troubleshoot [ˈtrʌblʃuːt] — устранять неполадки
6. software [ˈsɒftweə] — программное обеспечение

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~450 слов.
"Hey, Tom! Can you help me? The printer is broken again," said Emma, looking frustrated.

Tom sighed and walked over to the printer. "What's the problem this time?"

"It won't print anything. I've been trying for ten minutes," Emma said.

Tom looked at the printer screen. "It says 'Paper jam.' Did you check inside?"

Emma opened the printer and pulled out a crumpled piece of paper. "Oh, I see it now. How do I get it out?"

"Carefully pull it from both sides," Tom explained. "Don't tear it."

Emma removed the paper and closed the printer. "Okay, let's try again." She pressed the print button, and the printer started working.

"Thanks, Tom! You're a lifesaver," Emma said.

"No problem," Tom smiled. "By the way, did you know you can scan documents directly to your email from this printer? You don't need to use the computer."

"Really? How?" Emma asked.

"See this button here?" Tom pointed. "Press it, enter your email address on the screen, and it will scan and send the document automatically. It's much faster."

"Wow, I didn't know that," Emma said. "This will save me so much time."

Just then, their colleague Mike walked in. "Hey guys, my computer is running really slow today. Everything takes forever to load."

Tom turned to Mike. "Did you restart it?"

"Of course I did," Mike said. "I've restarted it three times already."

"Okay, let me check," Tom said, sitting at Mike's desk. He opened the task manager. "You have too many programs running in the background. Look, you've got Spotify, Slack, Chrome with 20 tabs, and three other apps all using memory."

Mike looked embarrassed. "I didn't realize that was a problem."

"It is," Tom said. "Close the programs you're not using. And you should also update your software. Sometimes old versions cause performance issues."

Mike closed some apps and updated his software. "It's already faster! Thanks, Tom."

"You should call IT support if it happens again," Tom advised. "They can troubleshoot more serious problems and maybe upgrade your RAM if needed."

"Will do," Mike said. "You really know your stuff about office technology."

Tom laughed. "I've been working here for five years. I've seen every equipment problem you can imagine."

Emma smiled. "We're lucky to have you. You save us so much time and frustration."

"Anytime," Tom said. "Just remember: when technology doesn't work, first try the simple solutions — restart, check connections, read error messages. If that doesn't help, then call IT support."

Mike nodded. "Good advice. I'll remember that."

From that day, Emma and Mike tried to solve small tech problems themselves before calling for help. And they both learned new tricks to use the office equipment more efficiently.

Тема: № 25 Планирование дня и управление временем (Daily Planning and Time Management)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. schedule [ˈʃedjuːl] — расписание, график
2. to prioritize [praɪˈɒrɪtaɪz] — расставлять приоритеты
3. deadline [ˈdedlaɪn] — крайний срок
4. to procrastinate [prəˈkræstɪneɪt] — откладывать, медлить
5. productive [prəˈdʌktɪv] — продуктивный
6. to multitask [ˌmʌltiˈtɑːsk] — делать несколько дел одновременно

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~460 слов.
"Sarah, you always seem so organized," said Jake during their coffee break. "How do you manage to get everything done on time?"

Sarah smiled. "It wasn't always like this. A year ago, I was always stressed and missing deadlines. Then I learned some time management techniques."

"Can you teach me?" Jake asked. "I'm always procrastinating and then rushing at the last minute."

"Sure," Sarah said. "First, I plan my day the night before. I write down all the tasks I need to do and prioritize them."

"How do you prioritize?" Jake asked.

"I use the Eisenhower Matrix," Sarah explained. "I divide tasks into four categories: urgent and important, important but not urgent, urgent but not important, and neither urgent nor important. I focus on the urgent and important tasks first."

"That makes sense," Jake said. "But what about multitasking? I try to do several things at once to save time."

Sarah shook her head. "That's actually a mistake. Multitasking makes you less productive. Your brain has to switch between tasks, and you lose focus. It's better to do one thing at a time and do it well."

Jake looked surprised. "Really? I thought multitasking was a good skill."

"Studies show it's not," Sarah said. "Instead, try time blocking. You allocate specific time slots for specific tasks. For example, from 9 to 11, you work on the report. From 11 to 11:30, you check emails. This way, you stay focused."

"What about interruptions?" Jake asked. "People always come to my desk with questions."

"Set boundaries," Sarah advised. "Use headphones when you need to focus. Or put a sign on your desk that says 'Do not disturb until 11 AM.' People will respect it."

Jake wrote everything down. "This is great. What about breaks? I feel guilty when I take them."

"Breaks are essential," Sarah said. "Use the Pomodoro technique: work for 25 minutes, then take a 5-minute break. After four cycles, take a longer 15-30 minute break. This keeps your brain fresh and productive."

"I'll try this starting tomorrow," Jake said.

The next day, Jake planned his schedule the night before. He prioritized his tasks, used time blocking, and took regular breaks. He didn't check his phone every five minutes, and he told his colleagues when he was in focus mode.

At the end of the day, Jake was amazed. "Sarah, I finished all my tasks and I'm not even tired! This is incredible."

Sarah smiled. "See? Time management is not about working harder. It's about working smarter."

A month later, Jake's manager noticed the change. "Jake, your productivity has improved a lot. What changed?"

Jake explained his new system. His manager was impressed and asked him to share his techniques with the team.

Jake realized that good time management is not just about getting more done. It's about reducing stress, having energy for important things, and having time for your personal life.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What problem did Jake have with time management?
2. What is the Eisenhower Matrix?
3. Why did Sarah say multitasking is a mistake?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Jake asked Sarah how she stays... (organized) and meets deadlines.
Sarah said she plans her day the night before and... (prioritizes) tasks.
She uses the... (Eisenhower Matrix) to divide tasks by urgency and importance.
Jake asked about... (multitasking), but Sarah said it makes you less... (productive).
She suggested... (time blocking) — allocating specific time for specific tasks.
To avoid interruptions, Sarah advised setting... (boundaries) like using headphones.
She also recommended the... (Pomodoro technique) — 25 minutes work, 5 minutes break.
Jake tried the system and finished all his tasks without feeling... (tired).
His manager noticed his improved... (productivity) and asked him to share with the team.
Jake realized that time management is about working... (smarter), not harder.

Тема: № 26 Встречи и совещания (Meetings and Conferences)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. agenda [əˈdʒendə] — повестка дня
2. to schedule [ˈʃedjuːl] — назначать, планировать
3. minutes [ˈmɪnɪts] — протокол (встречи)
4. to postpone [pəˈspəʊn] — откладывать, переносить
5. to wrap up [ræp ʌp] — завершать
6. action items [ˈækʃən ˈaɪtəmz] — задачи, действия

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~470 слов.
"Good morning, everyone. Let's start the meeting," said Rachel, the team leader. "I've sent you the agenda yesterday. Do you have any questions before we begin?"

"I have one," said Mark. "Item three says 'Budget review,' but we don't have the final numbers yet. Should we postpone this topic?"

Rachel checked her notes. "Good point. Let's move it to next week's meeting. We'll focus on the project timeline today."

"Sounds good," said Lisa. "By the way, can we start with a quick update from each team? I think some people are waiting for information from others."

"Agreed," Rachel said. "Let's go around the table. Mark, you start."

Mark spoke for two minutes about his progress. Then Lisa shared her update. When it was Tom's turn, he started talking about a problem he had with a client.

"Sorry to interrupt," Rachel said gently, "but can we save detailed discussions for later? Let's just get the main updates first."

"Right, sorry," Tom said. He quickly summarized his situation.

After everyone gave their updates, Rachel said, "Okay, let's discuss the project timeline. We're two weeks behind schedule. What can we do to catch up?"

The team brainstormed for fifteen minutes. They decided to hire two freelancers and extend working hours for the next month.

"Great," Rachel said. "Let's wrap up. I'll send the meeting minutes by the end of the day. Please check the action items and let me know if anything is unclear."

"What are the action items?" asked Lisa.

Rachel read from her notebook: "Mark will contact the freelancers by Wednesday. Lisa will prepare the revised timeline. Tom will call the client to explain the delay. And I'll update the budget spreadsheet."

"Perfect," everyone said.

"Any other business?" Rachel asked.

"Yes," said Tom. "Can we schedule a follow-up meeting for next Friday to check our progress?"

"Good idea," Rachel said. "I'll send the calendar invite. Alright, that's all for today. Thanks, everyone."

After the meeting, Lisa stayed behind. "Rachel, can I give you some feedback?"

"Sure," Rachel said.

"I think our meetings could be more efficient," Lisa said. "Sometimes we go off-topic, and people talk too long. Maybe we should set time limits for each agenda item?"

Rachel thought about it. "You're right. Let's try that. I'll allocate specific time for each topic and use a timer. Also, I'll send the agenda earlier so people can prepare."

"That would help," Lisa said. "And maybe we can have some meetings without laptops and phones? People get distracted."

"Good suggestion," Rachel agreed. "Let's try 'device-free' meetings once a week and see if it improves focus."

The next week, Rachel implemented these changes. She sent the agenda three days in advance, set time limits, and asked everyone to put away their devices.

The meetings became much shorter and more productive. People came prepared, stayed focused, and left with clear action items.

"Rachel, the meetings are so much better now," Mark said one day. "We get more done in 30 minutes than we used to in an hour."

Rachel smiled. "Thanks for the feedback, Lisa. It really made a difference."

The team realized that good meetings are not just about talking. They're about having a clear agenda, staying focused, respecting time, and leaving with concrete action items.

She suggested setting... (time limits) for each agenda item.
Rachel also decided to send the agenda... (earlier) and have device-free meetings.
The meetings became shorter and more... (productive).

Тема: № 27 Телефонные разговоры (Phone Calls)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. to pick up [pɪk ʌp] — брать трубку
2. to put on hold [pʊt ɒn həʊld] — ставить на удержание
3. to get cut off [ɡet kʌt ɒf] — прерываться (о связи)
4. voicemail [ˈvɔɪsmeɪl] — голосовая почта
5. to dial [daɪəl] — набирать номер
6. to clarify [ˈklærɪfaɪ] — уточнять, прояснять

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~430 слов.
"Hello? Is this Mr. Davis?" Anna asked, holding her phone tightly. She had been trying to reach the client all morning.

"Yes, this is he. Who is calling?" a tired voice answered.

"Hi, Mr. Davis. This is Anna from Bright Solutions. I’m calling to clarify the details of our upcoming project."

"Oh, right. The marketing project. Look, Anna, I’m in a meeting right now. Can I put you on hold for a minute?"

"Of course, take your time," Anna said politely. 

Soft music started playing in her ear. She waited for two minutes, but then suddenly, the line went dead. She got cut off. 

"Great," Anna muttered to herself. She decided to dial his number again immediately. 

This time, the phone rang five times and went straight to voicemail. "Hi, you’ve reached John Davis. Please leave a message after the beep."

"Hi, Mr. Davis, this is Anna again. Our call got cut off. Please call me back when you have a moment, or let me know a better time to reach you. My number is 555-0198."

Ten minutes later, her phone rang. It was Mr. Davis. 

"Anna? Sorry about that. My battery died. Let’s talk now. What did you need to clarify?"

"No problem at all," Anna smiled, relieved. "I just wanted to confirm the deadline for the first draft. Is it still next Friday?"

"Actually, can we move it to Wednesday? We have a board meeting on Thursday, and I need to review it beforehand."

Anna checked her calendar. "Wednesday is tight, but we can do it if you send us the final logo files by tomorrow morning."

"Done. I’ll have my assistant email them to you in an hour."

"Perfect. And one last thing: will you be available for a quick video call on Thursday to review the draft together?"

"Yes, Thursday at 2 PM works for me. I’ll send you a calendar invite."

"Excellent. Thank you for your time, Mr. Davis. Have a great day."

"You too, Anna. Bye."

Anna hung up and wrote down the new deadline in her planner. She realized that phone calls could be stressful, especially with bad connections, but being polite, patient, and clear always helped to solve the problem and keep the client happy.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)

Anna agreed, on the condition that he sends the... (logo files) by tomorrow.
They also scheduled a video call for... (Thursday at 2 PM).

Тема: № 28 Деловое письмо (Business Writing / Emails)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. subject line [ˈsʌbdʒɪkt laɪn] — тема письма
2. attachment [əˈtætʃmənt] — вложение
3. to forward [ˈfɔːwəd] — пересылать
4. to follow up [ˈfɒləʊ ʌp] — напоминать, уточнять (после действия)
5. concise [kənˈsaɪs] — краткий, лаконичный
6. typo [ˈtaɪpəʊ] — опечатка

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~440 слов.
"Hey, Mark, do you have a minute?" Sarah asked, rolling her chair over to Mark’s desk. 

"Sure, what’s up?" Mark replied, taking his eyes off the screen.

"I need you to read this email before I send it to the CEO. It’s about the Q3 budget, and I don’t want to make any mistakes."

Mark leaned in and read the screen. "Okay, let’s see. First, your subject line just says 'Budget'. That’s too vague. He gets hundreds of emails a day. Make it concise and specific, like 'Q3 Budget Report – Action Required'."

"Good point," Mark’s colleague nodded, typing the change. "What about the body of the email?"

"It’s a bit long," Mark said. "CEOs don’t have time to read three paragraphs. Get straight to the point. Say: 'Please find attached the Q3 budget report. The main highlight is a 10% cost reduction. Let me know if you have any questions.'"

Sarah deleted the extra text. "Much better. Wait, did I forget the attachment again?" She checked the bottom of the email. "Oh no, I almost did! Thanks for catching that."

"No problem. Also, watch out for typos. I saw one in the second paragraph. You wrote 'manger' instead of 'manager'."

Sarah blushed. "Oops. Fixed. Is the tone okay? I don’t want to sound too demanding."

"The tone is fine," Mark assured her. "It’s professional and polite. But remember to follow up if he doesn’t reply in two days. You can just forward the original email and add a short note: 'Just bringing this to the top of your inbox'."

"That’s a great tip," Sarah said, hitting the 'Send' button with confidence. "I always worry about sounding pushy when I follow up."

"It’s not pushy if it’s polite and necessary," Mark smiled. "Business emails are all about clarity, respect for the reader’s time, and double-checking your work."

Later that afternoon, Sarah’s phone buzzed. It was an email from the CEO. 

"Mark! Look!" she whispered excitedly. 

Mark read the short reply: 'Thank you, Sarah. The report looks great. Approved.'

"See? I told you," Mark said, giving her a high-five. "Clear subject line, concise message, correct attachment, and no typos. Perfect business email."

Sarah smiled. She realized that writing a good business email wasn’t about using fancy words. It was about being clear, professional, and making the recipient’s life easier.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why did Sarah ask Mark to read her email?
2. What did Mark suggest changing in the subject line?
3. What almost happened before Sarah sent the email?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Sarah asked Mark to check her email about the... (Q3 budget).
Mark said the subject line was too vague and should be more... (concise / specific).
He also told her to make the body of the email shorter and get straight to the... (point).
Sarah realized she almost forgot the... (attachment).
Mark also pointed out a... (typo) in the second paragraph.
He advised her to... (follow up) politely if the CEO didn’t reply in two days.
Sarah sent the email and quickly got an... (approval) from the CEO.
She realized that good business emails are about clarity and respecting the reader’s... (time).

Тема: № 29 Работа в команде (Teamwork)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. to collaborate [kəˈlæbəreɪt] — сотрудничать
2. to delegate [ˈdelɪɡeɪt] — делегировать, поручать
3. reliable [rɪˈlaɪəbl] — надежный
4. to compromise [ˈkɒmprəmaɪz] — идти на компромисс
5. shared goal [ʃeəd ɡəʊl] — общая цель
6. to support [səˈpɔːt] — поддерживать

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~450 слов.
"Guys, we have a huge problem," Ben said, rubbing his temples. "The client moved the presentation deadline to tomorrow morning. I can’t finish the slides and the data analysis by myself."

Alex and Chloe gathered around Ben’s desk. They were a small marketing team, and they had a shared goal: to win this new client. 

"Don’t panic," Alex said calmly. "We are a team. We will collaborate and figure this out. Let’s look at what needs to be done."

Chloe pulled up a chair. "Ben, you are the best at data analysis. If you focus only on the numbers, I can take over the slide design. I already have the templates ready."

Ben looked relieved. "Are you sure? I don’t want to dump my work on you."

"It’s not dumping, it’s delegating," Chloe smiled. "We support each other. That’s what teamwork is about."

"Okay," Ben agreed. "I’ll finish the data charts by 4 PM. Chloe, can you have the first draft of the slides ready by 6 PM?"

"Yes, but I need the charts first," Chloe said. 

"Deal," Ben said. 

Alex stepped in. "I’ll handle the spoken part of the presentation. I’ll write the script and rehearse it tonight. Also, I’ll order dinner for all of us so we don’t waste time going to the cafeteria."

"You’re a lifesaver, Alex," Ben said. 

They worked hard for the next four hours. There was a moment of tension when Chloe didn’t like the color scheme Ben chose for the charts. 

"Ben, the blue is too dark. It won’t show up on the projector," she pointed out.

"But the client’s brand color is dark blue," Ben argued. 

"Let’s compromise," Alex suggested. "Use dark blue for the titles, but make the data lines bright yellow or white so they are visible."

"Good idea," Ben and Chloe agreed. 

By 8 PM, the presentation was ready. They rehearsed it together twice. Alex’s script was smooth, Chloe’s slides looked professional, and Ben’s data was accurate and easy to understand. 

The next morning, the client was extremely impressed. "This is exactly what we were looking for," the client’s director said. "You clearly understand our needs."

After the meeting, the team went to a nearby cafe to celebrate. 

"We did it!" Chloe cheered, clinking her coffee cup with Ben’s. 

"I couldn’t have done it without you both," Ben admitted. "I was ready to give up yesterday."

"That’s the power of a reliable team," Alex said. "When one person struggles, the others step in. We have different skills, but we share the same goal."

Ben smiled. He realized that teamwork wasn’t just about working in the same room. It was about trusting each other, communicating openly, and being ready to help when things got tough.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What was the problem Ben faced at the beginning of the story?
2. How did the team divide the work?
3. What compromise did they reach about the chart colors?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Ben was stressed because the client moved the... (deadline) to tomorrow.
Alex and Chloe decided to... (collaborate) to reach their shared goal.
Chloe offered to take over the... (slide design) so Ben could focus on data.
Alex volunteered to write the... (script) and ordered dinner for the team.
When they disagreed on the chart colors, Alex suggested they... (compromise).
They finished the presentation by 8 PM and... (rehearsed) it together.
The next morning, the client was very... (impressed) with their work.
Ben realized that a... (reliable) team supports each other and communicates openly.

Тема: № 30 Переговоры и убеждение (Negotiation and Persuasion)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. to negotiate [nɪˈɡəʊʃieɪt] — вести переговоры
2. win-win situation [wɪn wɪn ˌsɪtʃuˈeɪʃn] — ситуация, выгодная для обеих сторон
3. to convince [kənˈvɪns] — убеждать
4. to offer a discount [ˈɒfə ə ˈdɪskaʊnt] — предлагать скидку
5. to meet halfway [miːt ˈhɑːfweɪ] — идти на взаимные уступки
6. objection [əbˈdʒekʃn] — возражение

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~460 слов.
David sat across the table from Mrs. Lee, the purchasing manager of a large retail chain. He was trying to sell her company’s new software, but the negotiation was not going smoothly.

"David, I like your product," Mrs. Lee said, crossing her arms. "But your price is 20% higher than your main competitor. I have an objection to this. Why should I pay more?"

David stayed calm. He knew that getting defensive would not help him convince her. "I completely understand your concern, Mrs. Lee. Price is always an important factor. However, our software includes 24/7 technical support and free monthly updates, which the competitor does not offer."

Mrs. Lee nodded slowly. "That’s true. But my budget is strict. I can’t just approve a 20% increase."

David thought for a moment. He needed to find a win-win situation. "What if we meet halfway?" he suggested. "If you sign a two-year contract instead of a one-year contract, I can offer you a 15% discount on the total price. This brings the cost much closer to your budget, and you still get all our premium support features."

Mrs. Lee raised an eyebrow. "A 15% discount for a two-year commitment? Let me do the math." She typed some numbers into her tablet. 

"Okay," she said after a minute. "That does fit within my budget. But what about the implementation? Will your team train our staff for free?"

"Usually, training costs extra," David admitted. "But to show our commitment to this partnership, I will include three days of free on-site training for your team."

Mrs. Lee smiled slightly. "Now that is a good offer."

"Great," David said, sliding the revised contract across the table. "Shall we sign it today?"

"Yes, let’s do it," Mrs. Lee said, picking up her pen. 

Later that day, David called his manager. "We got the deal," he said proudly. 

"Excellent work, David!" his manager replied. "How did you overcome the price objection?"

"I listened to her concerns, didn’t argue, and offered a creative solution," David explained. "A longer contract for a discount, plus free training. It was a win-win situation. She got the price she needed, and we secured a long-term client."

"That’s the art of negotiation," his manager said. "You didn’t just sell a product; you built a relationship."

David hung up the phone, feeling a great sense of achievement. He realized that successful negotiation is not about defeating the other person. It is about listening carefully, understanding their needs, and finding a solution that makes both sides happy.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What was Mrs. Lee’s main objection to David’s product?
2. What solution did David propose to meet halfway?
3. What extra benefit did David offer to close the deal?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, David was trying to sell software to Mrs. Lee, but she had an... (objection) about the high price.
David stayed calm and explained that their product includes 24/7... (support).
To find a... (win-win situation), David suggested they meet halfway.
He offered a 15%... (discount) if she signed a two-year... (contract).
Mrs. Lee checked the math and agreed it fit her... (budget).
She also asked if staff training would be free, and David... (offered) three days of free on-site training.
Mrs. Lee signed the... (contract) immediately.
David’s manager praised him for building a... (relationship), not just selling a product.
David realized that good negotiation is about listening and finding a solution that makes... (both sides) happy.

Тема: № 31 Электронная переписка и деловые письма (Email Correspondence and Business Letters)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. reply all [rɪˈplaɪ ɔːl] — ответить всем
2. thread [θred] — цепочка писем, ветка обсуждения
3. etiquette [ˈetɪket] — этикет
4. to attach [əˈtætʃ] — прилагать (файл)
5. CC / BCC [ˌsiː ˈsiː / ˌbiː ˌsiː ˈsiː] — копия / скрытая копия
6. to clarify [ˈklærɪfaɪ] — уточнять, прояснять

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~440 слов.
"Leo, do you have a minute?" Sarah, the office manager, asked, standing by Leo’s desk with a slight smile.

Leo looked up from his computer, feeling a sudden wave of panic. "Yes, Sarah. Is everything okay?"

"Well," Sarah began, "I saw the email you sent to the entire department this morning. The one about the broken coffee machine."

Leo’s face turned red. "Oh no. Did I do something wrong?"

"Not wrong, exactly," Sarah said kindly, pulling up a chair. "But you used 'Reply All'. The original email was sent to all 50 employees. Your reply, which just said 'I know, it’s so annoying!', went to everyone’s inbox."

Leo groaned and put his head in his hands. "I’m so sorry. I didn’t think. I just wanted to agree with Mark."

"It’s a common mistake," Sarah reassured him. "But it’s a great opportunity to talk about email etiquette. Let’s go over a few quick rules so you feel more confident."

"Please," Leo said. "I don’t want to be 'that guy' who spams everyone’s inbox."

Sarah nodded. "Rule number one: always check the 'To' field before hitting send. If you only need to answer the sender, just click 'Reply', not 'Reply All'."

"Got it," Leo said, taking notes. "What about CC and BCC? I never really understood the difference."

"Good question," Sarah explained. "Use CC (Carbon Copy) when you want to keep someone in the loop, but they don’t need to take action. Use BCC (Blind Carbon Copy) when you’re sending an email to a large group of people who don’t know each other, to protect their privacy."

"That makes sense," Leo said. "And what about long email threads? Sometimes I get lost in them."

"If a thread gets too long or complicated, it’s better to pick up the phone or walk over to the person’s desk to clarify things," Sarah advised. "Email is great for attaching documents or keeping a record, but it’s terrible for long debates."

"Wow, I had no idea there was so much to it," Leo admitted. "Thank you for explaining this to me, Sarah. I really appreciate it."

"No problem at all," Sarah smiled, standing up. "We all make mistakes when we’re new. Just remember: before you send, pause and think, 'Does everyone really need to see this?'"

Later that day, Leo needed to ask a question about a project. He looked at the email, hesitated, and clicked 'Reply' instead of 'Reply All'. He attached the file, wrote a concise message, and hit send. 

He felt a small sense of pride. He was already becoming a more professional communicator, one email at a time.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What mistake did Leo make in his morning email?
2. What is the difference between CC and BCC according to Sarah?
3. What does Sarah suggest doing if an email thread gets too complicated?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Leo accidentally used... (Reply All) to answer an email about the coffee machine.
Sarah explained that this is a common mistake but taught him about email... (etiquette).
She told him to always check the 'To' field and use... (Reply) instead of Reply All when possible.
She explained that... (CC) is for keeping people in the loop, while BCC protects privacy.
For long or complicated... (threads), Sarah suggested calling or talking in person to... (clarify).
Leo thanked Sarah and later successfully sent an email, making sure to... (attach) the file and write a concise message.
He learned to pause and think before hitting... (send).

Тема: № 32 Волонтерство и благотворительность (Volunteering and Charity)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. to donate [dəʊˈneɪt] — жертвовать, делать пожертвование
2. charity [ˈtʃærəti] — благотворительность
3. fundraiser [ˈfʌndreɪzə] — мероприятие по сбору средств
4. to organize [ˈɔːɡənaɪz] — организовывать
5. community service [kəˈmjuːnəti ˈsɜːvɪs] — общественная работа, волонтерство
6. to make a difference [meɪk ə ˈdɪfrəns] — иметь значение, менять ситуацию к лучшему

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~450 слов.
"Look at this, Maya," Sam said, showing her a post on his phone. "The local animal shelter is going to close next month if they don’t raise enough money."

Maya looked at the screen and frowned. "That’s terrible! They helped my cat last year. We have to do something."

"I know," Sam agreed. "But what can we do? We’re just students. We don’t have a lot of money to donate."

Maya thought for a moment, her eyes lighting up. "We don’t need money to start. We can organize a fundraiser! A bake sale or a charity run in the park."

Sam smiled. "I like the idea of a bake sale. Everyone loves food. But how do we get people to come?"

"We can use social media," Maya said enthusiastically. "We’ll create an event page, design some cool posters, and ask our friends to share it. We can also ask the local bakery to donate some extra cookies."

"Okay, I’m in," Sam said. "Let’s make a plan. I’ll handle the social media and posters. You talk to the bakery and the shelter."

For the next two weeks, Maya and Sam worked hard. Maya visited the shelter and spoke with the manager, who was thrilled and promised to bring three adoptable dogs to the event. Sam designed bright, eye-catching posters and posted them all over the university campus.

The day of the fundraiser arrived. It was a sunny Saturday in the central park. Maya and Sam set up a long table with cakes, cookies, and lemonade. A small sign read: "All proceeds go to the City Animal Shelter."

At first, only a few people stopped by. Maya felt a bit nervous. "What if nobody buys anything?" she whispered to Sam.

"Just give it time," Sam said confidently. 

Soon, a group of students from their university walked by. "Hey, Sam! Is this for the shelter?" one of them asked. 

"Yes! Buy a cookie and save a puppy," Sam joked. 

The group laughed and bought several boxes of cookies. Word spread quickly. Within two hours, the table was almost empty. The shelter manager arrived with the dogs, and people gathered around to pet them and take photos. 

By the end of the day, Maya and Sam counted the money. They had raised over $500! 

"We did it!" Maya cheered, hugging Sam. "We actually made a difference."

The shelter manager came over, looking emotional. "Thank you both so much. This money will buy food and medicine for the animals for the next two months. You saved us."

Maya and Sam looked at each other, tired but incredibly happy. They realized that community service isn’t about how much money you have. It’s about having a good idea, working together, and caring enough to take action.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why did Maya and Sam decide to take action?
2. What kind of fundraiser did they choose to organize?
3. How much money did they raise by the end of the day?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Sam showed Maya a post about the local... (animal shelter) closing down.
They decided to... (organize) a bake sale to help.
Sam handled social media and posters, while Maya talked to the... (bakery) and the shelter.
On the day of the... (fundraiser), they set up a table in the park.
At first, Maya was nervous, but soon many... (students) came to buy food.
The shelter manager brought adoptable... (dogs), which attracted more people.
By the end of the day, they raised over... ($500).
They realized that... (community service) is about working together and caring enough to take action.

Тема: № 33 Поиск работы (Job Hunting)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. job board [dʒɒb bɔːd] — сайт по поиску работы
2. to tailor [ˈteɪlə] — адаптировать, подстраивать (под требования)
3. networking [ˈnetwɜːkɪŋ] — нетворкинг, установление связей
4. rejection [rɪˈdʒekʃn] — отказ
5. to apply [əˈplaɪ] — подавать заявку, откликаться
6. to follow up [ˈfɒləʊ ʌp] — напоминать о себе, уточнять статус

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~460 слов.
Elena stared at her laptop screen and sighed heavily. "That’s the tenth rejection email this month, David. I’m starting to think nobody wants to hire me."

David, her older brother, walked into the room with two cups of tea. "Let me see. What did you apply for this time?"

"Three marketing assistant positions on a popular job board," Elena said, rubbing her eyes. "I sent the exact same resume to all of them."

David sat down and shook his head. "Elena, that’s your first mistake. You can’t just send the same generic resume to every company. You need to tailor your resume for each specific job description."

"But that takes so much time!" Elena complained.

"It does," David agreed. "But it’s worth it. If the job description asks for 'social media experience', make sure those words are at the top of your resume. Show them you’re exactly what they’re looking for."

Elena nodded slowly. "Okay, I’ll try that. But what about the other ninety applications I sent? Silence. Nothing."

"That brings me to my second point: networking," David said. "Applying online is like throwing a message in a bottle into the ocean. Most jobs are filled through connections. Who do you know in the marketing industry?"

Elena thought for a moment. "Well, my former professor, Dr. Evans, works as a consultant for a few agencies. And my friend Clara just started working at a digital firm."

"Perfect!" David said. "Don’t just ask them for a job. Ask them for advice. Send a polite message saying, 'Hi Clara, I see you’re working at XYZ Company. I’m currently looking for marketing roles and would love to hear about your experience there. Could we grab a coffee next week?'"

Elena typed the message to Clara right away. "What if she says no?"

"Then you follow up politely in a week, or you move on to the next person," David said. "Networking is a numbers game, just like applying. But the response rate is much higher."

Two days later, Elena met Clara for coffee. Clara was friendly and gave her great advice about the company culture. More importantly, Clara said, "Actually, we have an unadvertised opening on my team. Let me forward your tailored resume to the hiring manager."

Elena’s heart skipped a beat. "Really? Thank you so much, Clara!"

A week later, Elena got an email. It wasn’t a rejection. It was an invitation for an interview. 

She called David immediately, her voice shaking with excitement. "It worked! The networking and the tailored resume actually worked!"

"I never doubted it," David laughed. "See? Job hunting isn’t just about clicking 'apply'. It’s about strategy, personalization, and building relationships."

Elena smiled, feeling a new wave of confidence. She was no longer just sending resumes into the void. She was actively managing her career.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why was Elena getting so many rejection emails?
2. What two main pieces of advice did David give Elena?
3. How did Clara help Elena get an interview?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Elena was frustrated because she received her tenth... (rejection) email.
David told her not to send the same generic resume, but to... (tailor) it for each job.
He also advised her to start... (networking) instead of just using a job board.
Elena decided to contact her friend Clara to ask for... (advice), not a job directly.
Clara agreed to meet for coffee and offered to forward Elena’s resume for an unadvertised... (opening).
A week later, Elena received an invitation for an... (interview).
She realized that job hunting requires... (strategy) and building relationships, not just clicking 'apply'.

Тема: № 34 Собеседование на работу (Job Interview - Behavioral Focus)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. behavioral question [bɪˈheɪvjərəl ˈkwestʃən] — поведенческий вопрос (о прошлом опыте)
2. strength [streŋθ] — сильная сторона
3. weakness [ˈwiːknəs] — слабая сторона
4. STAR method [stɑː ˈmeθəd] — метод STAR (Ситуация, Задача, Действие, Результат)
5. to demonstrate [ˈdemənstreɪt] — демонстрировать, показывать
6. to reflect [rɪˈflekt] — размышлять, делать выводы

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~470 слов.
"Okay, Alex, let’s practice," said Maria, his career coach. "Imagine I’m the hiring manager. Tell me about a time you failed at work and how you handled it."

Alex froze. "Failed? I don’t want to tell them I failed! That will make me look bad."

Maria shook her head. "No, Alex. This is a classic behavioral question. They don’t expect you to be perfect. They want to see how you reflect on your mistakes and learn from them. You need to use the STAR method."

"The STAR method?" Alex asked, taking out his notebook.

"Yes," Maria explained. "S is for Situation: briefly describe the context. T is for Task: what was your goal? A is for Action: what specific steps did you take? And R is for Result: what was the outcome, and what did you learn?"

"Okay, let me try," Alex said. He thought for a moment. "Last year, I was managing a small project. The Situation was that we had a tight deadline. The Task was to deliver a report to the client by Friday."

"Good start," Maria encouraged. "What was the Action?"

"Well," Alex continued, "I tried to do everything myself because I didn’t want to bother my team. But by Thursday, I realized I couldn’t finish it. So, my Action was to immediately call a meeting, explain the delay, and delegate parts of the report to my colleagues."

"Excellent," Maria smiled. "And the Result?"

"The Result was that we submitted the report on Saturday morning, one day late. The client was a bit annoyed, but they appreciated our honesty and the high quality of the work. I learned that delegation is a strength, not a weakness, and that asking for help early is better than hiding a problem."

Maria clapped her hands. "Perfect! That was honest, structured, and showed great self-awareness. You demonstrated that you can solve problems under pressure."

Two days later, Alex sat in the interview room at TechGlobal. The hiring manager, Mr. Davis, looked at his notes. 

"Alex, your technical skills are impressive. But I’d like to ask a behavioral question. Tell me about a time you had a conflict with a team member. How did you resolve it?"

Alex took a deep breath. He remembered Maria’s advice. 

"Sure," Alex began calmly. "In my previous role, the Situation was that my colleague and I disagreed on the design of a presentation. The Task was to create a unified final version for the CEO."

Mr. Davis nodded, listening carefully.

"My Action was to invite him for a coffee. Instead of arguing, I asked him to explain his perspective. I realized his design was actually better for the data visualization. So, we combined my text structure with his charts."

"And the Result?" Mr. Davis asked.

"The Result was a highly successful presentation. The CEO praised our teamwork. I learned that listening to different opinions leads to a better final product."

Mr. Davis smiled and wrote something on his notepad. "That’s a very mature answer, Alex. Thank you."

Alex walked out of the building feeling confident. He realized that a job interview wasn’t an interrogation. It was a conversation where he could demonstrate his real experience, using clear structure and honest reflection.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why did Alex initially not want to talk about failure in the interview?
2. What does the STAR method stand for?
3. How did Alex resolve the conflict with his colleague in his final answer?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Alex was practicing for an interview with his coach, Maria.
She told him to use the... (STAR method) for behavioral questions.
S stands for... (Situation), T for Task, A for... (Action), and R for Result.
Alex practiced an answer about a time he tried to do everything himself and had to... (delegate).
In the real interview, Mr. Davis asked about a... (conflict) with a team member.
Alex explained the Situation and Task, then described his Action: inviting the colleague for... (coffee) and listening.
The Result was a successful presentation and a lesson about... (teamwork / listening).
Alex realized that an interview is a chance to... (demonstrate) real experience honestly.

Тема: № 35 Профессии и карьера (Professions and Career)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. profession [prəˈfeʃn] — профессия
2. career path [kəˈrɪə pɑːθ] — карьерный путь
3. to pursue [pəˈsjuː] — стремиться к, заниматься (чем-либо)
4. demanding [dɪˈmɑːndɪŋ] — требовательный, сложный
5. rewarding [rɪˈwɔːdɪŋ] — приносящий удовлетворение, стоящий усилий
6. to switch careers [swɪtʃ kəˈrɪəz] — сменить профессию

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~430 слов.
"Julia, you’ve been staring at that spreadsheet for an hour," Mark said, rolling his chair over to her desk. "Are you okay?"

Julia sighed and closed her laptop. "Honestly, Mark? I’m not sure. I’ve been an accountant for five years. It’s a stable profession, but I feel completely empty."

Mark looked surprised. "Really? But you’re so good at it. You just got promoted!"

"I know," Julia replied. "But I’ve been thinking about my career path. I don’t want to be a senior accountant. I want to pursue something creative. I’m thinking about switching careers to graphic design."

Mark raised his eyebrows. "Graphic design? That’s a big change. Isn’t that field very demanding and competitive?"

"It is," Julia admitted. "But I’ve been taking online courses for the last six months. I love it. It feels so rewarding when I create something from scratch. Yesterday, I designed a logo for a friend’s small business, and she absolutely loved it."

"Wow, I had no idea," Mark said. "So, what’s your plan? Are you going to quit tomorrow?"

Julia laughed. "No, I’m not crazy! I need to build a portfolio first. I plan to work part-time as a designer while keeping my accounting job for financial stability. It will be hard, but I have to try."

Mark smiled warmly. "I think that’s a brave and smart decision. If you ever need help reviewing your resume for design jobs, let me know. I know a few people in the marketing department who might give you some feedback."

"Thank you, Mark. That means a lot," Julia said, feeling a sudden wave of relief. 

For the first time in months, she didn’t feel trapped. She realized that a career isn’t a straight line. It’s okay to change direction if your current profession doesn’t make you happy. As she packed her bag to go home, she opened her design folder instead of her accounting files. She was ready to start her new journey.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why did Julia feel empty despite her promotion?
2. What new profession does Julia want to pursue?
3. What is Julia’s plan to switch careers safely?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Julia told Mark she felt empty in her current... (profession) as an accountant.
She decided she wanted to change her... (career path) and become a graphic designer.
Mark warned her that the new field could be... (demanding) and competitive.
However, Julia found her online courses very... (rewarding).
She decided not to quit immediately, but to build a... (portfolio) first.
She plans to work part-time in design while keeping her... (accounting job) for stability.
Mark offered to help her by connecting her with people in... (marketing).
Julia realized it is okay to... (switch careers) if you are not happy.

Тема: № 36 Навыки и опыт (Skills and Experience)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. hard skills [hɑːd skɪlz] — профессиональные (жесткие) навыки
2. soft skills [sɒft skɪlz] — гибкие навыки (коммуникация, адаптивность)
3. hands-on experience [hændz ɒn ɪkˈspɪəriəns] — практический опыт
4. to adapt [əˈdæpt] — адаптироваться
5. to master [ˈmɑːstə] — осваивать в совершенстве
6. track record [træk ˈrekɔːd] — послужной список, репутация

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~440 слов.
"David, I don’t think I’m ready for the new project," Emma said, looking stressed. She was a junior developer, and her manager had just assigned her to lead a small team.

"Why not?" David asked, leaning against the doorframe. "You have excellent hard skills. You master Python and SQL better than anyone on the team."

Emma shook her head. "But I don’t have hands-on experience in leadership. What if I can’t adapt to managing people? My track record is only in coding, not in managing."

David smiled and pulled up a chair. "Emma, let me tell you a secret. Hard skills get you the job, but soft skills get you promoted."

"Soft skills?" Emma asked. "You mean things like communication?"

"Exactly," David said. "Think about your last project. When the server crashed, who stayed calm, organized the team, and communicated clearly with the client? You did. That’s leadership. That’s a crucial soft skill."

Emma thought about it. "I guess I did handle that well. But I still feel nervous."

"That’s normal," David reassured her. "Nobody is born a perfect manager. You will learn by doing. I will be your mentor for the first month. We will have weekly check-ins, and I will give you honest feedback."

"Really? You would do that?" Emma’s eyes widened.

"Of course," David said. "My goal is to help you grow. Focus on listening to your team, being clear about expectations, and admitting when you don’t know something. Those are the soft skills that matter most."

Emma took a deep breath. "Okay. I’ll try. But if I make a mistake, promise you won’t fire me."

David laughed. "I promise. Mistakes are how we gain experience. Just don’t make the same mistake twice."

Over the next few weeks, Emma faced challenges. Sometimes meetings ran late, and sometimes team members disagreed. But she remembered David’s advice. She listened, adapted her approach, and communicated openly. 

At the end of the month, the project was a success. The client sent a thank-you email specifically praising Emma’s organization and clarity. 

David stopped by her desk. "See? I told you. You have the experience; you just needed to believe in your soft skills."

Emma smiled, feeling a new sense of confidence. She realized that technical knowledge is important, but the ability to work with people is what truly makes a professional.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why was Emma worried about leading the new project?
2. What is the difference between hard skills and soft skills according to David?
3. How did David offer to help Emma?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Emma felt she wasn’t ready to lead because she lacked... (hands-on experience) in management.
David reminded her that she already has excellent... (hard skills) in coding.
He explained that while hard skills get you the job,... (soft skills) get you promoted.
David gave an example of how Emma showed leadership by staying... (calm) during a server crash.
He offered to be her... (mentor) and give weekly feedback.
Emma agreed to try, focusing on listening and... (communication).
The project was a success, and the client praised Emma’s... (organization).
Emma realized that working well with... (people) is just as important as technical knowledge.

Тема: № 37 Конфликты на работе (Workplace Conflicts)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. to resolve [rɪˈzɒlv] — разрешать (конфликт)
2. misunderstanding [ˌmɪsʌndəˈstændɪŋ] — недоразумение
3. to confront [kənˈfrʌnt] — столкнуться, открыто обсудить проблему
4. constructive criticism [kənˈstrʌktɪv ˈkrɪtɪsɪzm] — конструктивная критика
5. to apologize [əˈpɒlədʒaɪz] — извиняться
6. common ground [ˈkɒmən ɡraʊnd] — общая почва, взаимопонимание

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~450 слов.
The atmosphere in the office was tense. Alex and Ben had been arguing all morning about the new website design. 

"I told you, the blue button is too small!" Alex said, his voice rising. "Users won’t see it!"

"And I told you," Ben replied, crossing his arms, "the client specifically asked for a minimalist design. A big button ruins the aesthetic."

They stared at each other, neither willing to back down. The rest of the team worked in silence, feeling uncomfortable. 

Finally, Alex stood up. "Look, this isn’t working. Let’s take a break. Coffee? My treat."

Ben hesitated, then nodded. "Fine. Let’s talk."

They walked to the nearby cafe. The fresh air helped them calm down. 

"Ben, I’m sorry I raised my voice," Alex started. "I’m just stressed about the deadline. But I genuinely believe the user experience is suffering."

Ben sighed. "I apologize too, Alex. I was defensive. I spent three days on that design, and it felt like you were attacking my work, not the button."

"I wasn’t attacking you," Alex said gently. "I was trying to give constructive criticism. We both want this project to succeed."

"I know," Ben admitted. "So, how do we resolve this? The client wants minimalism, but you want usability."

Alex thought for a moment. "What if we find some common ground? We can keep the minimalist background, but make the button a bright, contrasting color, like orange. It will be small, but highly visible."

Ben’s eyes lit up. "That’s actually a great idea. It satisfies the client’s aesthetic and your usability concern. Let’s try it."

They returned to the office and updated the design together. When they showed it to the rest of the team, everyone agreed it was the best version yet. 

Later that day, their manager, Sarah, stopped by. "I heard you two were arguing earlier. Is everything okay now?"

"Yes, Sarah," Alex said. "We had a misunderstanding, but we talked it out. We found a solution that works for everyone."

Sarah smiled. "I’m glad to hear that. Conflicts are normal in creative work. The important thing is how you resolve them. You handled it like true professionals."

Alex and Ben looked at each other and smiled. They realized that avoiding conflict only makes things worse. By confronting the issue calmly, listening to each other, and being willing to apologize, they turned a frustrating argument into a productive collaboration.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What were Alex and Ben arguing about?
2. How did Alex start to resolve the conflict at the cafe?
3. What compromise (common ground) did they find for the button design?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Alex and Ben had a tense argument about the... (website design).
Alex felt the button was too small, while Ben wanted to keep the... (minimalist) style.
They decided to take a break and talk at a... (cafe).
Alex was the first to... (apologize) for raising his voice.
Ben admitted he was defensive because he felt his work was being attacked, not just the... (button).
Alex explained he was trying to give... (constructive criticism).
They found... (common ground) by making the small button a bright, contrasting color.
Their manager praised them for handling the... (conflict) like professionals.
They learned that confronting issues calmly helps to... (resolve) problems effectively.

Тема: № 38 Повышение квалификации и обучение (Professional Development and Training)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. workshop [ˈwɜːkʃɒp] — семинар, практическое занятие
2. to upskill [ʌpˈskɪl] — повышать квалификацию
3. certification [ˌsɜːtɪfɪˈkeɪʃn] — сертификат, подтверждение квалификации
4. mentor [ˈmentɔː] — наставник
5. to attend [əˈtend] — посещать
6. feedback [ˈfiːdbæk] — обратная связь

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~440 слов.
"Sarah, you’ve been with the company for two years," Tom, the department head, said during her annual review. "Your work is solid. But if you want that Senior Analyst promotion next year, you need to upskill."

Sarah nodded. "I know. I’ve been thinking about that. But I’m not sure where to start. There are so many courses online."

Tom smiled. "I actually have a suggestion. Next month, there’s a three-day advanced data analytics workshop in the city. It’s intensive, but it ends with a professional certification. I think you should attend."

"A workshop?" Sarah asked, feeling a bit nervous. "Will the company pay for it?"

"Yes, it’s part of our professional development budget," Tom confirmed. "But there’s a condition. After the workshop, you’ll need to present what you learned to the rest of the team. And I’ll assign Mark as your mentor to help you apply the new skills to your current projects."

Sarah’s eyes widened. "Mark? The senior analyst? That’s amazing!"

"He’s agreed to it," Tom said. "He thinks you have great potential. So, are you in?"

"Absolutely," Sarah said, feeling a surge of motivation. "Thank you, Tom."

The workshop was challenging. Sarah spent three long days learning complex software and statistical methods. She was tired, but she took detailed notes and asked many questions. 

When she returned to the office, she felt overwhelmed. "Mark, I don’t know if I can present this to the team. It’s too complicated," she confessed.

Mark laughed. "Don’t worry. We’ll practice. Let’s break it down into simple steps. Your goal isn’t to teach them everything, but to show them how this new tool can save us time."

Over the next two weeks, Mark and Sarah worked together. He gave her constructive feedback on her presentation slides and helped her run a test analysis on real company data. 

Finally, the presentation day arrived. Sarah stood in front of the team, took a deep breath, and began. She spoke clearly, showed practical examples, and answered questions confidently. 

When she finished, the room erupted in applause. 

"Excellent job, Sarah," Tom said, smiling. "That was exactly the kind of initiative we need. The promotion next year is yours if you keep this up."

Sarah beamed. She looked at Mark, who gave her a thumbs-up. She realized that professional development isn’t just about getting a certificate. It’s about stepping out of your comfort zone, learning from a mentor, and sharing that knowledge to help the whole team grow.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What did Tom suggest Sarah do to get a promotion?
2. What was the condition for the company paying for the workshop?
3. How did Mark help Sarah prepare for her presentation?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Tom told Sarah she needed to... (upskill) to become a Senior Analyst.
He suggested she attend an advanced data analytics... (workshop) to get a... (certification).
The company agreed to pay, but Sarah had to present her new knowledge to the... (team).
Tom assigned Mark to be her... (mentor).
The workshop was challenging, and Sarah felt... (overwhelmed) at first.
Mark helped her by practicing and giving... (feedback) on her presentation.
Sarah presented confidently, and the team gave her... (applause).
Tom confirmed that her... (promotion) was possible if she kept up the good work.
Sarah realized that learning is about stepping out of your comfort zone and... (sharing) knowledge.

Тема: № 39 Бизнес-этикет (Business Etiquette)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. punctual [ˈpʌŋktʃuəl] — пунктуальный
2. dress code [dres kəʊd] — дресс-код
3. to shake hands [ʃeɪk hændz] — пожимать руку
4. small talk [smɔːl tɔːk] — светская беседа, разговор ни о чем
5. to interrupt [ˌɪntəˈrʌpt] — перебивать
6. polite [pəˈlaɪt] — вежливый

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~430 слов.
It was Liam’s first day at a large international company. He was excited but a bit nervous. He wanted to make a good impression, but he wasn’t entirely sure about the rules of business etiquette.

He arrived at the office at 9:15 AM. His manager, Sophia, was already at her desk. 
"Good morning, Liam," she said with a warm smile. "I’m glad you’re here. Just a quick tip for the future: we value being punctual. Meetings usually start exactly at 9:00 AM."

Liam’s face turned red. "I’m so sorry, Sophia. The traffic was terrible. It won’t happen again."

"It’s okay, it’s your first day," she reassured him. "Now, let me introduce you to the team. Remember to make eye contact, smile, and shake hands firmly. And please, call everyone by their first name. We are quite informal here."

They walked to the open-plan office. Sophia introduced Liam to his colleagues. When an older colleague, Mr. Davis, started explaining his role, Liam got too excited and interrupted him. 
"Actually, I read about that software!" Liam said loudly. 

Mr. Davis stopped talking and looked a bit annoyed. Sophia gently stepped in. "Liam, it’s very important to listen actively and not interrupt when someone is speaking. It shows respect."

"I apologize, Mr. Davis," Liam said quickly. "Please, continue."

Later, they went to the break room for coffee. Sophia noticed Liam was wearing a t-shirt and ripped jeans. 
"Liam, I should have mentioned this earlier," she said kindly. "We have a business casual dress code. A t-shirt is fine for Fridays, but for the rest of the week, we expect a collared shirt or a blouse."

"Oh, I didn’t know," Liam said, feeling embarrassed. "I’ll change tomorrow."

"Don’t worry," Sophia smiled. "And one last thing: when you meet clients or senior managers, always start with a little small talk. Ask about their weekend or the weather before jumping straight into business. It builds trust."

Liam took out his notebook and wrote everything down. "Thank you, Sophia. I really appreciate your honesty. I want to be polite and professional."

"You’re doing great," she said. "Etiquette is just about making others feel comfortable and respected. You’ll get the hang of it in no time."

By the end of the week, Liam was arriving at 8:45 AM, dressed in smart casual clothes, and actively listening to his colleagues. He realized that business etiquette wasn’t about strict, old-fashioned rules. It was about common sense, respect, and professionalism.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why did Sophia remind Liam about being punctual?
2. What mistake did Liam make when Mr. Davis was speaking?
3. What did Sophia advise Liam to do before talking about business with clients?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Liam was late on his first day, and Sophia reminded him to be... (punctual).
She taught him to make eye contact and... (shake hands) firmly.
Liam made a mistake when he decided to... (interrupt) Mr. Davis.
Sophia explained that it is important to be... (polite) and listen actively.
She also noticed his t-shirt and explained the company’s... (dress code).
Finally, she advised him to start meetings with some... (small talk).
Liam wrote everything down and realized that etiquette is about... (respect).

Тема: № 40 Офисная лексика (Office Vocabulary)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. cubicle [ˈkjuːbɪkl] — офисная перегородка, кабинка
2. break room [breɪk ruːm] — комната отдыха
3. to clock in/out [klɒk ɪn/aʊt] — отмечаться о приходе/уходе с работы
4. stationery [ˈsteɪʃənri] — канцелярские принадлежности
5. open-plan office [ˈəʊpən plæn ˈɒfɪs] — офис открытой планировки
6. to schedule [ˈʃedjuːl] — планировать, назначать

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~440 слов.
"Excuse me, are you the new marketing assistant?" asked a friendly voice. 

Emma turned around from her desk. A tall man with a cup of coffee was smiling at her. "Yes, I’m Emma. I just started today."

"Welcome to the team! I’m Jack, I work in sales. My cubicle is right across from yours," he said, pointing to the desk opposite hers. "How are you settling in?"

"It’s a bit overwhelming," Emma admitted. "This open-plan office is so big! I’m still trying to figure out where everything is."

Jack laughed. "Don’t worry, everyone feels that way on the first day. Let me give you a quick tour. First, do you know how to clock in and out?"

"I think so," Emma said. "I use the ID card on the scanner by the main entrance, right?"

"Exactly," Jack confirmed. "Just remember to do it every morning and evening, or HR will send you an automated email."

They walked down the hallway. "This is the break room," Jack said, opening a door. "We have a microwave, a coffee machine, and a fridge. Please label your food with your name and the date. We had an incident last month with someone’s missing sandwich."

Emma laughed. "Noted! What about supplies? I need some pens and sticky notes."

"Ah, stationery," Jack said. "We have a supply closet on the third floor. You can take what you need, but there’s a sign-out sheet on the door. Just write your name and the item."

"That’s easy enough," Emma said, feeling more relaxed. "One more question: how do I book a meeting room? My manager asked me to schedule a brainstorming session for tomorrow."

"You can do that through the company intranet," Jack explained. "Just go to the 'Rooms' tab, check the availability, and click 'Book'. It will automatically send a calendar invite to everyone you add."

"Wow, that’s very efficient," Emma said. "Thank you so much, Jack. You’ve saved me a lot of time."

"No problem at all," Jack smiled. "Oh, and one pro tip: the coffee machine makes the best espresso, but it takes about two minutes to heat up. Be patient!"

"Good to know," Emma replied. 

As Jack walked back to his desk, Emma sat down and organized her new pens and sticky notes. She realized that learning the office vocabulary and routines wasn’t just about memorizing words. It was about understanding how the workplace functions and feeling like a true part of the team.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Where is Jack’s cubicle located?
2. How does Emma clock in and out of work?
3. What is the rule for taking stationery from the supply closet?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):

He explained that she needs to use her ID card to... (clock in/out).
He showed her the... (break room) and warned her to label her food.
When Emma asked for pens, Jack told her about the... (stationery) closet and the sign-out sheet.
To book a room, Emma needs to use the company... (intranet) to schedule the meeting.
Jack also gave her a pro tip about the... (coffee machine).
Emma realized that learning office routines helps her feel like part of the... (team).
