Тема: № 41 Корпоративная культура (Corporate Culture)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. values [ˈvæljuːz] — ценности
2. transparency [trænsˈpærənsi] — прозрачность, открытость
3. work-life balance [wɜːk laɪf ˈbæləns] — баланс между работой и личной жизнью
4. hierarchy [ˈhaɪərɑːki] — иерархия
5. to empower [ɪmˈpaʊə] — давать полномочия, вдохновлять
6. team-building [ˈtiːm ˌbɪldɪŋ] — тимбилдинг, сплочение команды

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~450 слов.
"Welcome to InnovateTech, David," said Helen, the HR director, as she handed him a company handbook. "Before you meet your team, I want to talk about our corporate culture."

David nodded, curious. "I’ve heard great things about this company. What makes it so special?"

Helen smiled. "It starts with our core values. We believe in transparency, innovation, and a healthy work-life balance. We don’t like strict hierarchy here. In fact, our CEO’s door is always open. You can talk to anyone, at any level."

"Really?" David asked, surprised. "In my last job, I needed three approvals just to speak to a department head."

"Exactly," Helen said. "We want to empower our employees. We trust you to make decisions and take ownership of your projects. We don’t micromanage."

"That sounds amazing," David said. "But what about the work-life balance? I’ve heard tech companies can be very demanding."

"We work hard, but we also rest well," Helen explained. "We offer flexible working hours. If you need to pick up your kids from school or go to a doctor’s appointment, just let your manager know. We care about results, not about how many hours you sit at your desk."

David felt a wave of relief. "That’s exactly what I was looking for."

"Also," Helen continued, "we invest a lot in team-building. Every quarter, we have a company retreat or a fun activity, like a cooking class or a hiking trip. It helps everyone connect outside of work."

"Is it mandatory?" David joked.

Helen laughed. "No, but it’s highly encouraged! Most people love it. Oh, and we have a 'Fail Forward' policy."

"Fail forward? What does that mean?"

"It means we encourage risk-taking," Helen said seriously. "If you try a new idea and it fails, we don’t punish you. We analyze what went wrong, learn from it, and move on. We believe that without failure, there is no true innovation."

David smiled broadly. "Helen, I think I’m going to love it here."

"I’m glad to hear that," she said, standing up. "Your manager, Mark, will show you to your desk now. Feel free to ask him any questions. Remember, transparency goes both ways. If something isn’t working for you, speak up."

As David followed Mark down the bright, plant-filled hallway, he felt a strong sense of belonging. He realized that corporate culture wasn’t just words on a website. It was a living, breathing environment that shaped how people worked, communicated, and grew together.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What are the three core values of InnovateTech?
2. How does the company support work-life balance?
3. What does the "Fail Forward" policy mean?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Helen explained that the company doesn’t have a strict... (hierarchy).
They believe in... (transparency) and keeping the CEO's door open.
The company wants to... (empower) employees and trusts them to make decisions.
They support... (work-life balance) by offering flexible working hours.
They also invest in... (team-building) activities every quarter.
The "Fail Forward" policy encourages risk-taking and learning from... (failure).
David realized that corporate culture is a living environment that shapes how people... (work and grow).

Тема: № 42 Деловые встречи (Business Meetings - External Focus)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. to host [həʊst] — принимать (гостей), проводить (мероприятие)
2. venue [ˈvenjuː] — место проведения
3. to facilitate [fəˈsɪlɪteɪt] — облегчать, направлять (обсуждение)
4. minutes [ˈmɪnɪts] — протокол встречи
5. follow-up [ˈfɒləʊ ʌp] — последующие действия, обратная связь после встречи
6. to address [əˈdres] — обращаться к, решать (проблему/вопрос)

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~460 слов.
Anna took a deep breath and looked around the conference room. Today, she was going to host an important meeting with a potential client from Germany, Mr. Schmidt. 

"Is everything ready?" she asked her assistant, Tom.

"Yes, Anna," Tom replied. "The venue is booked, the projector is working, and I’ve printed the agenda and the presentation handouts. The coffee and water are on the table."

"Perfect," Anna said. "Remember, my goal is to facilitate the discussion, not to talk the whole time. I need to listen to his needs."

At 10:00 AM, Mr. Schmidt arrived. Anna greeted him warmly and shook his hand. "Welcome to our office, Mr. Schmidt. I hope you had a good trip."

"Thank you, Anna. The trip was fine," he replied politely. 

They sat down, and Anna started the meeting. "As you can see from the agenda, we will first review your current challenges, then present our software solution, and finally discuss the budget and timeline."

Mr. Schmidt nodded. "That sounds good. But I must address one concern immediately. Your competitor offers a similar product for 15% less. Why should we choose you?"

Anna didn’t panic. She had prepared for this. "That’s a very fair question, Mr. Schmidt. Our initial price might be slightly higher, but our software includes 24/7 dedicated support and free integration with your existing systems. Your competitor charges extra for those services. In the long run, our solution will save you money and time."

Mr. Schmidt leaned back and thought for a moment. "I see. That is a valid point. Can you guarantee the integration will be completed within two months?"

"Yes, absolutely," Anna said confidently. "We have a dedicated team for that."

The rest of the meeting went smoothly. Mr. Schmidt asked detailed questions, and Anna answered them clearly, facilitating a productive dialogue. 

After an hour, Anna wrapped up the meeting. "Thank you for your time, Mr. Schmidt. I will send you the meeting minutes and a detailed proposal by tomorrow morning."

"I look forward to it," he said, standing up. "It was a very productive discussion."

After he left, Tom walked into the room. "That was impressive, Anna. You handled his question about the price perfectly."

"Thank you, Tom," Anna smiled, feeling relieved. "Now, let’s draft the follow-up email. We need to send the proposal while the details are still fresh in his mind."

Anna realized that hosting a successful business meeting wasn’t just about having a nice venue or a fancy presentation. It was about preparation, active listening, addressing concerns honestly, and ensuring a clear, professional follow-up.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):

2. What was Mr. Schmidt’s main concern about the price?
3. What did Anna promise to send by the next morning?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):

Anna explained that their price includes 24/7 support and free... (integration).
Mr. Schmidt agreed it was a valid point and asked about the... (timeline).
After the meeting, Anna promised to send the... (minutes) and a proposal.
She realized that a successful meeting requires preparation, listening, and a clear... (follow-up).

Тема: № 43 Работа с документами (Working with Documents)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. to file [faɪl] — подшивать, хранить документы
2. confidential [ˌkɒnfɪˈdenʃl] — конфиденциальный
3. to sign [saɪn] — подписывать
4. hard copy [hɑːd ˈkɒpi] — бумажная копия
5. to archive [ˈɑːkaɪv] — отправлять в архив
6. deadline [ˈdedlaɪn] — крайний срок

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~430 слов.
"Rachel, can you help me find the Johnson contract?" asked Mr. Peterson, the senior partner at the law firm. "I need to review it before the meeting at 3 PM."

Rachel, a new paralegal, felt a wave of panic. "Of course, Mr. Peterson. I’ll find it right away."

She sat down at her desk and opened the filing system. The firm had recently switched to a digital archive, but some old documents were still in physical folders. She checked the digital system first. Nothing.

"Maybe it’s in the basement archive," she muttered to herself. 

She ran downstairs to the storage room. Rows of metal shelves filled the space, each labeled with years and client names. She found the section for 2019 and started scanning the folders.

"Come on, come on," she whispered, her fingers flying over the tabs. "Johnson, Johnson..."

Finally, she found it: a thick blue folder labeled "Johnson Corp – 2019." She opened it carefully. Inside were dozens of pages, all marked "Confidential."

She carried the folder back upstairs, but on her way, she bumped into Tom, a colleague from another department.

"Hey Rachel, what’s that?" Tom asked, glancing at the folder.

"It’s the Johnson contract," she said, holding it close. "Mr. Peterson needs it."

Tom raised his eyebrows. "That’s confidential, right? You shouldn’t be carrying it around like that. What if you drop it?"

Rachel looked at the folder again. He was right. She had been so focused on finding it that she forgot about security.

"Good point," she said. "I’ll put it in a sealed envelope."

She went back to her desk, placed the folder in a large envelope, and wrote "Confidential – For Mr. Peterson Only" on the outside. Then she walked to Mr. Peterson’s office and knocked.

"Come in," he said.

Rachel handed him the envelope. "Here’s the Johnson contract, Mr. Peterson. I found it in the basement archive."

Mr. Peterson opened the envelope and checked the contents. "Perfect. Thank you, Rachel. By the way, did you make a hard copy for yourself?"

"No, I didn’t think I needed one," she admitted.

"Always make a hard copy when you handle important documents," he advised. "That way, if something happens to the original, you have a backup. And remember, confidential documents should never leave your sight unless they’re sealed."

Rachel nodded, taking out her notebook. "Got it. Thank you, Mr. Peterson."

As she walked back to her desk, she realized that working with documents wasn’t just about organizing files. It was about understanding security, being prepared, and taking responsibility for every piece of paper that passed through her hands.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)

Тема: № 44 Деловые письма (Formal Business Letters)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. formal letter [ˈfɔːml ˈletə] — официальное письмо
2. to complain [kəmˈpleɪn] — жаловаться
3. refund [ˈriːfʌnd] — возврат денег
4. to demand [dɪˈmɑːnd] — требовать
5. compensation [ˌkɒmpenˈseɪʃn] — компенсация
6. to resolve [rɪˈzɒlv] — решать, урегулировать

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~440 слов.
"Mrs. Johnson, I need your help," said Emma, a small business owner, sitting across from her lawyer. "I ordered 500 custom-made boxes for my bakery from a supplier, and they arrived damaged. All of them."

Mrs. Johnson, a senior lawyer specializing in business disputes, nodded. "I’m sorry to hear that. Did you contact the supplier?"

"Yes, I called them immediately," Emma said, frustrated. "They said it was the shipping company’s fault and refused to help. I’ve lost a lot of money, and I can’t pack my products."

"Let me see the contract," Mrs. Johnson said, extending her hand.

Emma handed her a printed document. Mrs. Johnson read it carefully for a few minutes.

"I see the problem," she said finally. "The contract says the supplier is responsible for the goods until they are delivered to your location. That means they are liable for the damage, not the shipping company."

"So what can I do?" Emma asked.

"You need to write a formal letter of complaint," Mrs. Johnson explained. "It should be professional, clear, and specific. Start with the facts: when you ordered, what was delivered, and how it was damaged. Then, state what you want: a full refund or replacement boxes within a specific deadline."

Emma took out her laptop. "Can you help me draft it?"

"Of course," Mrs. Johnson said. "Let’s start. Write: 'Dear Sir or Madam, I am writing to formally complain about the order number 4567, delivered on March 15th.'"

Emma typed quickly. "Okay, now what?"

"Next, describe the problem. Write: 'Upon inspection, we discovered that all 500 boxes were damaged and unusable. This has caused significant disruption to our business operations.'"

Emma continued typing. "And now I need to demand a refund, right?"

"Yes," Mrs. Johnson said. "Write: 'We demand a full refund of $2,500 or replacement boxes within 10 business days. If this matter is not resolved by [date], we will consider further legal action.'"

Emma finished the letter and read it aloud. "That sounds very strong."

"It needs to be," Mrs. Johnson said. "But remain professional. Don’t use emotional language. Just stick to the facts and your demands."

Emma sent the letter that afternoon. Three days later, she received a response. The supplier apologized and offered a full refund plus compensation for the delay.

"Thank you, Mrs. Johnson," Emma called her. "It worked! They agreed to everything."

"I’m glad to hear that," Mrs. Johnson replied. "A well-written formal letter can resolve many problems without going to court. Just remember: always keep a copy of everything you send."

Emma smiled. She realized that formal business letters weren’t just about complaining. They were a powerful tool to protect your rights and resolve disputes professionally.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What problem did Emma have with the box supplier?
2. What did Mrs. Johnson say about who was responsible for the damage?
3. What did Emma demand in her formal letter?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Emma ordered 500 custom boxes, but they arrived... (damaged).
The supplier blamed the... (shipping company) and refused to help.
Mrs. Johnson checked the contract and said the supplier was... (liable).
She advised Emma to write a formal... (letter of complaint).
The letter included the facts, the problem, and a... (demand).
Emma demanded a full... (refund) or replacement within 10 days.
The supplier responded in three days and offered a refund plus... (compensation).
Emma learned that formal letters are a powerful tool to... (resolve disputes).

Тема: № 45 Тайм-менеджмент (Time Management - Advanced)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. procrastination [prəˌkræstɪˈneɪʃn] — прокрастинация, откладывание дел
2. to prioritize [praɪˈɒrɪtaɪz] — расставлять приоритеты
3. distraction [dɪˈstrækʃn] — отвлечение
4. to delegate [ˈdelɪɡeɪt] — делегировать
5. burnout [ˈbɜːnaʊt] — выгорание
6. to schedule [ˈʃedjuːl] — планировать

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~450 слов.
"Kate, you look exhausted," said her colleague, James, as they met in the office kitchen. "Are you sleeping at all?"

Kate sighed and poured herself a third cup of coffee. "Barely. I have three projects due next week, and I haven’t even started on the most important one. I keep procrastinating."

James leaned against the counter. "I used to be the same way. But I learned a few time management techniques that really helped. Want to hear them?"

"Please," Kate said. "I’m desperate."

"First," James began, "you need to identify your biggest distraction. For me, it was my phone. I was checking social media every five minutes. I started using an app that blocks distracting websites during work hours. It made a huge difference."

Kate nodded. "That makes sense. My distraction is email. I feel like I have to respond immediately."

"Try batching," James suggested. "Check your email only three times a day: morning, lunch, and late afternoon. You’ll be surprised how much time you save."

"Okay, I’ll try that," Kate said. "But what about the three projects? How do I decide which one to start with?"

"Use the Eisenhower Matrix," James explained. "Divide your tasks into four categories: urgent and important, important but not urgent, urgent but not important, and neither. Start with the urgent and important ones. If something is important but not urgent, schedule it. If it’s urgent but not important, delegate it if possible."

Kate thought for a moment. "The client presentation is urgent and important. The team report is important but not urgent. And answering random emails is urgent but not important."

"Exactly," James said. "Focus on the presentation first. And for the report, block out two hours tomorrow morning when you’re most productive. Turn off your phone and close your email."

"What if I still feel overwhelmed?" Kate asked.

"Take breaks," James advised. "Use the Pomodoro technique: 25 minutes of focused work, then a 5-minute break. After four cycles, take a longer break. And most importantly, don’t forget to schedule downtime. If you don’t rest, you’ll burn out."

Kate wrote everything down in her notebook. "Thank you, James. I’m going to try this starting today."

The next week, Kate implemented James’s advice. She blocked distracting websites, batched her email, and used the Eisenhower Matrix to prioritize. She also took regular breaks and scheduled time to relax in the evenings.

By Friday, she had finished all three projects and even had time to review next week’s tasks. She felt energized instead of exhausted.

"James, you’re a genius," she told him on Monday morning. "I actually slept eight hours last night."

James smiled. "See? Time management isn’t about working harder. It’s about working smarter and taking care of yourself."

Kate realized that managing time wasn’t just about getting more done. It was about reducing stress, avoiding burnout, and creating space for both work and life.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why was Kate feeling exhausted?
2. What did James suggest to deal with distractions?
3. What technique did Kate use to prioritize her projects?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Kate was exhausted because she had three projects and kept... (procrastinating).
James suggested using an app to block... (distractions) like social media.
He also advised her to... (batch) email checking to three times a day.
To prioritize, Kate used the... (Eisenhower Matrix).
She identified the client presentation as urgent and... (important).
James recommended the... (Pomodoro technique) for focused work.
He also emphasized the importance of scheduling... (downtime) to avoid burnout.
By Friday, Kate finished all projects and felt... (energized).
She realized that time management is about working... (smarter) and taking care of yourself.

Тема: № 46 Офисное оборудование (Office Equipment - Remote Work Focus)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. headset [ˈhedset] — гарнитура
2. webcam [ˈwebkæm] — веб-камера
3. bandwidth [ˈbændwɪdθ] — пропускная способность интернета
4. ergonomic [ˌɜːɡəˈnɒmɪk] — эргономичный
5. to troubleshoot [ˈtrʌblʃuːt] — устранять неполадки
6. backup [ˈbækʌp] — резервная копия

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~440 слов.
"Hey, Lisa, can you hear me?" asked Mark, his voice crackling through the laptop speakers. "You’re freezing on my screen."

Lisa adjusted her headset and sighed. "I can hear you, but the connection is terrible. I think my home internet bandwidth isn’t enough for video calls."

Mark, who was working remotely from another city, nodded. "I had the same problem when I first started working from home. Let me give you some advice on setting up a proper home office."

"Please," Lisa said. "I’m working from my kitchen table, and my back is killing me."

"First," Mark said, "invest in an ergonomic chair and a separate monitor. Working from a laptop screen for eight hours a day will ruin your posture. And get a good headset with noise cancellation. It makes a huge difference during calls."

Lisa took notes. "Okay, chair and headset. What about the internet?"

"Call your provider and upgrade your plan," Mark advised. "You need at least 50 Mbps for smooth video calls. And always have a backup plan. I keep my phone’s hotspot ready in case my internet goes down."

"Good idea," Lisa said. "What about the webcam? Mine is blurry."

"Buy an external webcam," Mark suggested. "The built-in ones are usually low quality. Also, make sure you have good lighting. Face a window or get a ring light. It makes you look much more professional on camera."

Lisa ordered the equipment online. A few days later, everything arrived. She set up her new workspace in the spare room: ergonomic chair, external monitor, HD webcam, and a ring light.

The next day, she had a team meeting. "How do I look now?" she asked Mark.

"Much better!" he said. "Your image is clear, and the sound is perfect. No more freezing."

"Thanks for the advice," Lisa said. "One more thing: how do you handle file sharing? I’m worried about losing important documents."

"Use cloud storage," Mark explained. "Google Drive, Dropbox, or OneDrive. They automatically sync your files and create backups. And always keep a local backup on an external hard drive. Better safe than sorry."

Lisa set up cloud storage that afternoon. She uploaded all her important files and configured automatic backups.

A week later, her computer crashed unexpectedly. She panicked for a moment, then remembered Mark’s advice. She logged into her cloud account from her phone and accessed all her files.

"Mark, you saved my life," she texted him. "My computer died, but I didn’t lose anything."

"That’s the beauty of backups," Mark replied. "Always be prepared for the worst."

Lisa realized that working remotely required more than just a laptop. It required the right equipment, a good setup, and smart habits to stay productive and protected.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why was Lisa’s video connection freezing?
2. What equipment did Mark suggest for a better home office?
3. How did Lisa avoid losing her files when her computer crashed?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):

Mark suggested investing in an... (ergonomic) chair and external monitor.

Mark explained the importance of cloud storage and local... (backups).

Тема: № 47 Биржевые термины (Stock Market Terms)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. stock [stɒk] — акция
2. to invest [ɪnˈvest] — инвестировать
3. dividend [ˈdɪvɪdend] — дивиденд
4. portfolio [pɔːtˈfəʊliəʊ] — портфель (инвестиций)
5. to diversify [daɪˈvɜːsɪfaɪ] — диверсифицировать
6. volatile [ˈvɒlətaɪl] — волатильный, нестабильный

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~450 слов.
"Dad, I want to start investing," said 22-year-old Alex over dinner. "I’ve been reading about the stock market, and I think it’s time to put my savings to work."

His father, Robert, a financial advisor, smiled. "That’s a great decision, Alex. But before you buy your first stock, you need to understand the basics. What do you know so far?"

"Well," Alex began, "a stock is a share of ownership in a company. When you buy a stock, you become a partial owner. And if the company does well, the stock price goes up, and you can sell it for a profit."

"Correct," Robert said. "But there’s more to it. Some companies also pay dividends. That’s a portion of the company’s profits distributed to shareholders. It’s like getting regular income from your investment."

Alex nodded. "I’ve heard about that. So, should I just buy stocks in big companies like Apple or Tesla?"

Robert shook his head. "Not so fast. The stock market can be very volatile. Prices go up and down every day. If you put all your money in one stock and it crashes, you could lose everything. That’s why you need to diversify your portfolio."

"Diversify?" Alex asked.

"It means spreading your investments across different stocks, sectors, and even countries," Robert explained. "That way, if one company fails, the others can balance it out. Don’t put all your eggs in one basket."

Alex thought about this. "So I should buy stocks in technology, healthcare, energy, and maybe some international companies?"

"Exactly," Robert said. "And don’t forget about bonds. They’re less risky than stocks and can provide steady income. A good portfolio has a mix of both."

"What about timing the market?" Alex asked. "Should I wait for the prices to drop before buying?"

Robert laughed. "That’s what everyone tries to do, but it’s nearly impossible to predict. The best strategy is to invest regularly, no matter what the market is doing. It’s called dollar-cost averaging. You buy a fixed amount every month, regardless of the price."

Alex wrote everything down in his notebook. "This is a lot to take in. Where do I start?"

"Start small," Robert advised. "Open a brokerage account, deposit a small amount, and buy a few diversified stocks or an index fund. An index fund tracks the whole market, so it’s automatically diversified. And most importantly, be patient. Investing is a long-term game."

Alex smiled. "Thanks, Dad. I feel much more confident now."

"Remember," Robert said, "investing isn’t about getting rich quick. It’s about building wealth slowly and steadily over time."

Alex realized that the stock market wasn’t just about buying and selling stocks. It was about understanding risk, diversifying wisely, and having the patience to let your investments grow.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What is a stock according to Alex?
2. What did Robert say about dividends?
3. Why is it important to diversify a portfolio?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Alex told his dad he wanted to start... (investing) in the stock market.
Robert explained that a stock is a share of... (ownership) in a company.
Some companies pay... (dividends), which is a portion of profits.
Robert warned that the market can be... (volatile) and prices change daily.
He advised Alex to... (diversify) his portfolio across different sectors.
Robert also mentioned bonds as a less... (risky) investment.
Instead of timing the market, Robert suggested... (dollar-cost averaging).
He told Alex to start small with an index fund for automatic... (diversification).
Alex realized that investing is about building wealth... (slowly and steadily).

Тема: № 48 Виды активов (Types of Assets)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. asset [ˈæset] — актив
2. liquid [ˈlɪkwɪd] — ликвидный (легко convertible в деньги)
3. real estate [ˈriːl ɪˈsteɪt] — недвижимость
4. to appreciate [əˈpriːʃieɪt] — расти в цене
5. depreciation [dɪˌpriːʃɪˈeɪʃn] — обесценивание, износ
6. to generate income [ˈdʒenəreɪt ˈɪnkʌm] — генерировать доход

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~460 слов.
"Grandpa, what’s the difference between being rich and being wealthy?" asked 16-year-old Sophie during their weekend visit.

Her grandfather, a retired businessman, smiled. "That’s a very smart question, Sophie. Let me explain. Being rich means you have a lot of money right now. Being wealthy means you have assets that generate income and grow over time."

"Assets?" Sophie asked. "Like what?"

"An asset is anything that has value and can put money in your pocket," Grandpa explained. "There are different types. The most common are cash, real estate, stocks, and businesses."

Sophie thought for a moment. "So if I have $1,000 in my savings account, that’s an asset?"

"Yes, but it’s a liquid asset," Grandpa said. "That means you can access it immediately. But cash doesn’t grow much. Inflation will eat away its value over time."

"What about real estate?" Sophie asked. "Mom and Dad own our house. Is that an asset?"

"It can be," Grandpa said. "Real estate usually appreciates over time, meaning it increases in value. And if you rent it out, it generates income. But a house is not a liquid asset. You can’t sell it quickly if you need cash."

Sophie nodded. "What about my car? Is that an asset?"

Grandpa chuckled. "Actually, a car is a liability, not an asset. It depreciates, which means it loses value over time. And it costs you money for gas, insurance, and maintenance. True assets put money in your pocket; liabilities take money out."

"So I should buy things that grow in value or generate income," Sophie summarized.

"Exactly," Grandpa said. "For example, if you buy stocks in a good company, they can appreciate and pay dividends. If you start a small business, it can generate profits. The key is to acquire assets that work for you, even when you’re sleeping."

Sophie looked thoughtful. "But how do I start? I don’t have much money."

"Start small," Grandpa advised. "Save a portion of your allowance and invest it. Buy a few shares of a company you believe in. Or save up and start a small side business, like selling handmade crafts online. The important thing is to begin early."

"What about education?" Sophie asked. "Is that an asset?"

Grandpa’s eyes lit up. "That’s the best asset of all! Your skills and knowledge can generate income for your entire life. No one can take that away from you."

Sophie smiled. "I think I understand now. Being wealthy isn’t about how much you spend. It’s about what you own and what it does for you."

"Exactly," Grandpa said. "Rich is about income. Wealthy is about assets. And the sooner you start building assets, the sooner you’ll achieve financial freedom."

Sophie realized that understanding the difference between assets and liabilities was the foundation of financial literacy. She decided to start saving and learning about investing right away.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What is the difference between being rich and being wealthy according to Grandpa?
2. Why is cash considered a liquid asset but not the best long-term investment?
3. Why did Grandpa say a car is a liability, not an asset?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Sophie asked her grandpa about the difference between being rich and... (wealthy).
Grandpa explained that an... (asset) is anything that has value and generates income.
Cash is a... (liquid) asset, but it doesn’t grow much due to inflation.
Real estate usually... (appreciates) over time and can generate rental income.
A car is a liability because it... (depreciates) and costs money to maintain.
Grandpa advised Sophie to buy things that grow in value or... (generate income).
He said the best asset is... (education) and skills.
Sophie realized that wealth is about what you... (own) and what it does for you.
She decided to start saving and learning about... (investing) early.

Тема: № 49 Офисные разговоры и small talk (Office Chitchat and Small Talk)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. small talk [smɔːl tɔːk] — светская беседа, разговор ни о чем
2. to break the ice [breɪk ði aɪs] — разрядить обстановку, растопить лед
3. to catch up [kætʃ ʌp] — обменяться новостями
4. awkward [ˈɔːkwəd] — неловкий
5. weather [ˈweðə] — погода
6. common ground [ˈkɒmən ɡraʊnd] — общая тема, взаимопонимание

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~420 слов.
It was 10:00 AM on a Tuesday, and the office coffee machine was humming. Sarah, a new marketing assistant, stood there nervously, waiting for her coffee. She didn’t know anyone well yet, and the silence felt awkward.

Suddenly, David, a senior designer, walked in. "Morning, Sarah! How’s your first week going?" he asked with a friendly smile.

Sarah relaxed a bit. "Hi, David. It’s going well, thanks! A bit overwhelming, but I’m learning a lot."

"I remember my first week," David laughed. "I spilled coffee on my keyboard on day two. So, you’re doing great. Do you have any plans for the weekend?"

Sarah was surprised but pleased. "Actually, yes! I’m going to a local art exhibition on Saturday. I love modern art."

David’s eyes lit up. "No way! Are you going to the new gallery on Main Street? I was just thinking about checking it out."

"Yes, that’s the one!" Sarah smiled. "Have you been there before?"

"Not yet, but I’ve heard great things. Maybe I’ll see you there," David said. "By the way, can you believe this rain? I thought spring was supposed to be sunny."

"I know, right?" Sarah agreed. "I had to take an umbrella today, and I almost forgot it on the bus."

They laughed, and the awkward silence was completely gone. David had successfully broken the ice. 

"Well, I better get back to my desk," David said, taking his mug. "It was great catching up with you, Sarah. If you need any help with the design software, just let me know."

"Thank you, David! I really appreciate it," Sarah replied.

As David walked away, Sarah felt much more comfortable. She realized that small talk wasn’t just empty chatter. It was a crucial tool for building relationships, finding common ground, and making the workplace feel friendly and welcoming. From that day on, she made an effort to say hello and ask about her colleagues' weekends.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why did Sarah feel awkward at the coffee machine?
2. How did David break the ice with Sarah?
3. What common interest did Sarah and David discover?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Sarah was a new employee and felt... (awkward) at the coffee machine.
David came in and asked her about her... (first week / weekend).
Sarah mentioned she was going to an... (art exhibition).
David was also interested in the new gallery on... (Main Street).
They also briefly discussed the rainy... (weather).
David successfully... (broke the ice) and offered help with design software.
Sarah realized that... (small talk) is a crucial tool for building relationships and finding... (common ground).

Тема: № 50 Рабочие встречи и обсуждение задач (Work Meetings and Task Discussion)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. stand-up meeting [stænd ʌp ˈmiːtɪŋ] — короткая летучка (стоя)
2. to assign a task [əˈsaɪn ə tɑːsk] — назначать задачу
3. progress report [ˈprəʊɡres rɪˈpɔːt] — отчет о прогрессе
4. to brainstorm [ˈbreɪnstɔːm] — проводить мозговой штурм
5. roadblock [ˈrəʊdblɒk] — препятствие, проблема
6. action item [ˈækʃən ˈaɪtəm] — конкретное действие, задача

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~440 слов.
"Alright team, let’s do a quick stand-up meeting," said Elena, the project manager, clapping her hands lightly. "It’s 9:30 AM. Let’s keep it to 15 minutes. Mark, you start."

Mark stood up from his desk. "Yesterday, I finished the first draft of the website copy. Today, I’ll review it with the design team. No roadblocks so far."

"Great," Elena noted. "Lisa, how is the mobile app development going?"

Lisa sighed slightly. "Well, I have a progress report. The coding is 80% done, but we hit a roadblock with the payment gateway integration. The API documentation from the vendor is unclear."

Elena frowned. "That’s a critical issue. We need to resolve it before Friday’s deadline. Let’s brainstorm solutions right now. Tom, you worked with this vendor before, right?"

Tom nodded. "Yes, I did. Their documentation is always messy. But I have a workaround. I can share my old code snippets with Lisa. It should save her at least two days of work."

Lisa looked relieved. "That would be amazing, Tom! Thank you."

"Perfect," Elena said, typing quickly on her laptop. "So, here are the action items. Tom, you will send the code snippets to Lisa by noon. Lisa, you will implement the workaround and test it by Thursday. Mark, you’ll proceed with the design review as planned. Any questions?"

"No, that’s clear," Lisa said.

"Sounds good to me," Mark added.

"Excellent. Let’s get to work," Elena smiled. "And remember, if you hit another roadblock, don’t wait until the next meeting. Message me immediately."

The team dispersed, feeling focused and aligned. Mark started his design review, Tom immediately opened his old project files to help Lisa, and Lisa began testing the new code. 

Later that afternoon, Lisa sent a message to the team chat: "Payment gateway is working perfectly! Thanks, Tom, your code saved the day."

Elena replied with a thumbs-up emoji. She realized that short, focused meetings weren’t a waste of time. When done right, they helped the team identify problems early, brainstorm solutions together, and leave with clear action items.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What is the purpose of a stand-up meeting?
2. What roadblock did Lisa report during the meeting?
3. How did the team resolve the payment gateway issue?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Elena started a quick... (stand-up meeting) at 9:30 AM.
Mark reported that he finished the website... (copy) with no roadblocks.
Lisa gave a... (progress report) but mentioned a problem with the payment gateway.
The team decided to... (brainstorm) a solution immediately.
Tom offered to share his old... (code snippets) to help Lisa.
Elena listed the... (action items) for everyone.
By the afternoon, Lisa confirmed the gateway was... (working).
Elena realized that short meetings help identify problems and create clear... (action items).

Тема: № 51 Электронная переписка и деловые письма (новая) (Email Correspondence - Advanced Scenarios)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. out of office reply [aʊt əv ˈɒfɪs rɪˈplaɪ] — автоответчик об отсутствии
2. to forward [ˈfɔːwəd] — пересылать (письмо)
3. urgent [ˈɜːdʒənt] — срочный
4. to decline an invitation [dɪˈklaɪn ən ˌɪnvɪˈteɪʃn] — вежливо отклонить приглашение
5. to RSVP [ˌɑːr es viː ˈpiː] — подтвердить или отклонить приглашение
6. thread [θred] — цепочка писем

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~430 слов.
"Hey, Alex, before you leave for your vacation, did you set up your out of office reply?" asked Maria, the office administrator.

Alex paused, his hand on his bag. "Oh no, I completely forgot! Thanks for reminding me." He quickly opened his laptop and typed: "Thank you for your email. I am currently out of the office with no access to email. I will return on Monday, June 12th. For urgent matters, please contact Maria at maria@company.com."

"Perfect," Maria said. "Now, about the client meeting next Tuesday. I sent you the calendar invite. Did you RSVP?"

Alex checked his inbox. "Yes, I saw it. But I have to decline the invitation. I’ll still be traveling on Tuesday morning. Can we reschedule it for Wednesday afternoon?"

"Let me check with the client," Maria said. She opened the email thread and hit 'Reply All'. She wrote: "Dear Mr. Smith, Alex is traveling on Tuesday. Would Wednesday at 2 PM work for you? Please let us know."

Within ten minutes, a reply popped up. "Wednesday at 2 PM is perfect. See you then."

"Great, I’ll update the calendar invite," Maria said. "Oh, and one more thing. Did you forward the Q2 financial report to the accounting department?"

Alex’s eyes widened. "I meant to do it yesterday! Let me do it right now." He found the file, attached it to a new email, and wrote a concise message: "Please find attached the Q2 financial report. Let me know if you have any questions." He double-checked the attachment and hit send.

"Done," Alex said, closing his laptop. "Is there anything else?"

"No, that’s everything," Maria smiled. "Have a great vacation!"

"Thanks, Maria. I’ll make sure my out of office reply is active before I walk out that door."

As Alex left the building, he felt a sense of relief. He realized that professional email management wasn’t just about writing well. It was about setting clear boundaries (like the out of office reply), communicating schedule changes politely (declining and rescheduling), and double-checking important actions (like forwarding attachments) before logging off.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What did Alex forget to do before his vacation?
2. Why did Alex have to decline the meeting invitation?
3. What did Alex do to ensure the accounting department got the report?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Maria reminded Alex to set up his... (out of office reply).
Alex typed a message stating he had no access to email and gave Maria as a contact for... (urgent) matters.
Maria asked if he had... (RSVPed) to the client meeting.
Alex had to... (decline the invitation) because he would be traveling.
Maria emailed the client to... (reschedule) for Wednesday, and the client agreed.
Alex realized he forgot to... (forward) the Q2 financial report.
He quickly attached the file and sent a... (concise) message.
Alex learned that email management requires setting boundaries and double-checking... (attachments).

Тема: № 52 Управление временем и продуктивность (Time Management and Productivity)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. to procrastinate [prəˈkræstɪneɪt] — откладывать дела на потом
2. distraction [dɪˈstrækʃn] — отвлечение
3. time blocking [taɪm ˈblɒkɪŋ] — блочное планирование времени
4. to delegate [ˈdelɪɡeɪt] — делегировать, поручать
5. efficient [ɪˈfɪʃnt] — эффективный
6. to focus [ˈfəʊkəs] — сосредотачиваться

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~450 слов.
"I can’t do this anymore," James groaned, putting his head on his desk. "I have three reports due tomorrow, and I’ve spent the last two hours scrolling through social media. I’m a master at procrastination."

His colleague, Anna, rolled her chair over. "James, you’re not a master at procrastination, you’re just overwhelmed. Let’s fix this together."

"How? I have zero focus," James replied.

"First, identify your biggest distraction," Anna said. "For you, it’s your phone. Put it in your drawer on silent mode."

James sighed but did it. "Okay, it’s in the drawer. Now what?"

"Now, we use time blocking," Anna explained. "Don’t look at the whole mountain of work. Break it down. Let’s block the next 45 minutes exclusively for Report A. No emails, no chatting, just Report A. Can you do 45 minutes?"

"Forty-five minutes I can handle," James said.

"Great. Set a timer. Go."

James opened the document and started typing. Without his phone buzzing, he found it surprisingly easy to focus. When the timer rang 45 minutes later, he had finished half of Report A.

"Wow, that actually worked," James said, stretching his arms.

"Told you," Anna smiled. "Now, take a 10-minute break. Get some water. Then we’ll do another block for Report B."

"What about Report C?" James asked. "I won’t have time for all three."

"That’s where delegation comes in," Anna said. "Can you ask Tom to handle the data collection for Report C? He’s faster at it anyway. You just review his work tomorrow morning."

James nodded. "You’re right. I’ll message him right after my break."

James followed Anna’s advice. He worked in focused 45-minute blocks, took short breaks, and delegated the data collection to Tom. By 6:00 PM, Reports A and B were completely finished, and Tom had already sent the data for Report C.

"Anna, you are a lifesaver," James said, packing his bag. "I feel so much more efficient today. I didn’t even feel stressed."

"Productivity isn’t about working 12 hours a day," Anna replied. "It’s about managing your energy, eliminating distractions, and working smart. You just proved that to yourself."

James walked out of the office feeling proud. He realized that beating procrastination wasn’t about sheer willpower. It was about using the right strategies, like time blocking and delegation, to make the work manageable.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why was James feeling overwhelmed at the beginning of the story?
2. What was James’s biggest distraction, and how did Anna suggest handling it?
3. How did James manage to finish his work by 6:00 PM?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, James was stressed because he had to... (procrastinate) and scroll social media instead of working.
Anna told him to remove his biggest... (distraction) by putting his phone in a drawer.
She introduced him to... (time blocking), suggesting he work for 45 minutes on one report.
James found it easy to... (focus) without interruptions.
For the third report, Anna suggested he... (delegate) the data collection to Tom.
James took short breaks and worked in focused... (blocks).
By 6:00 PM, he finished his work and felt much more... (efficient).
He learned that productivity is about managing energy and working... (smart).

Тема: № 53 Обратная связь и оценка работы (Feedback and Performance Review)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. feedback [ˈfiːdbæk] — обратная связь
2. constructive criticism [kənˈstrʌktɪv ˈkrɪtɪsɪzm] — конструктивная критика
3. strengths [streŋθs] — сильные стороны
4. areas for improvement [ˈeəriəz fər ɪmˈpruːvmənt] — зоны для роста, улучшения
5. performance review [pəˈfɔːməns rɪˈvjuː] — оценка эффективности работы
6. to acknowledge [əkˈnɒlɪdʒ] — признавать, отмечать

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~440 слов.
"Come in, Chloe, have a seat," said Mr. Davis, the department manager. "It’s time for your annual performance review."

Chloe sat down, feeling a bit nervous. "Thank you, Mr. Davis. I’m ready."

"First of all, I want to acknowledge your hard work this year," Mr. Davis began with a warm smile. "Your strengths are very clear. You are incredibly organized, and your presentation skills are outstanding. The client loved your pitch last month."

Chloe relaxed and smiled. "Thank you. I really enjoyed working on that project."

"However," Mr. Davis continued gently, "every performance review includes areas for improvement. I’ve noticed that you sometimes struggle with time management when you have multiple deadlines. You tend to take on too much and then rush at the end."

Chloe nodded thoughtfully. "You’re right. I find it hard to say no to new tasks, and then I get overwhelmed."

"Exactly," Mr. Davis said. "This is where constructive criticism comes in. I’m not saying you’re doing a bad job. I’m suggesting a way to make your excellent work even better. Have you considered using a task management tool or delegating smaller tasks to the junior team?"

"I’ve thought about it," Chloe admitted. "I think I need to prioritize better and communicate my workload to you earlier."

"That’s a fantastic goal," Mr. Davis said, writing it down. "Let’s make that one of your objectives for the next quarter. I will support you in this. If you feel overwhelmed, come to me, and we will adjust your tasks."

"Thank you, Mr. Davis. I appreciate the honest feedback. It’s very helpful," Chloe said sincerely.

"My door is always open," he replied. "You have a bright future here, Chloe. Keep up the great work on your strengths, and let’s tackle those areas for improvement together."

Chloe left the office feeling motivated, not defeated. She realized that a good performance review wasn’t about listing mistakes. It was a balanced conversation that acknowledged strengths, provided constructive criticism, and created a clear plan for professional growth. She immediately opened her laptop and downloaded a task management app.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What strengths did Mr. Davis acknowledge in Chloe’s work?
2. What area for improvement did he point out?
3. How did Chloe react to the constructive criticism?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Mr. Davis started the... (performance review) by acknowledging Chloe’s hard work.
He highlighted her... (strengths), like being organized and having great presentation skills.
Then, he gently mentioned an area for... (improvement): time management.
He explained that she takes on too much and... (rushes) at the end.
He offered... (constructive criticism) and suggested using a task management tool or delegating.
Chloe agreed and said she needs to... (prioritize) better and communicate her workload.
They made it a goal for the next... (quarter).
Chloe left feeling motivated, realizing that feedback is about balanced... (growth).

Тема: № 54 Удалённая работа и онлайн-коммуникация (Remote Work and Online Communication)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. remote work [rɪˈməʊt wɜːk] — удаленная работа
2. video call [ˈvɪdiəʊ kɔːl] — видеозвонок
3. to mute [mjuːt] — отключать звук (микрофон)
4. background noise [ˈbækɡraʊnd nɔɪz] — фоновый шум
5. screen sharing [skriːn ˈʃeərɪŋ] — демонстрация экрана
6. Wi-Fi connection [ˈwaɪ faɪ kəˈnekʃn] — соединение Wi-Fi

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~450 слов.
"Can everyone hear me?" asked Julia, adjusting her webcam. "I think my Wi-Fi connection is a bit unstable today."

"Loud and clear, Julia!" replied Mark from his home office. "But I think Sarah is on mute."

"Oh, sorry!" Sarah’s voice suddenly came through, accompanied by loud barking. "My dog is going crazy because the delivery guy is at the door. Let me mute myself for a second."

The team laughed. Remote work had its funny moments. 

"Alright, let’s start the video call," Julia said. "Today we need to review the new website layout. I’ll use screen sharing so you can see the design."

Julia clicked the 'Share Screen' button, but nothing happened. "Hmm, it’s not working. Let me try again."

"Maybe you need to grant the application permission in your settings," Mark suggested. "That happened to me last week."

"Good catch, Mark," Julia said. She quickly checked her settings, fixed the permission, and tried again. This time, the website layout appeared on everyone’s screens. "There we go! Can you see it?"

"Yes, perfect," Sarah said, her dog finally quiet. "I love the new color scheme. But the 'Contact Us' button is a bit too small on mobile."

"Good point," Julia noted. "I’ll make it larger. What about the loading speed? Mark, you mentioned last time it was a bit slow."

"I tested it this morning," Mark replied. "It’s much faster now. The developer optimized the images. Great job, team."

"Excellent," Julia smiled. "I’ll make the button adjustment today and send the updated link by 4 PM. Any other issues?"

"No, I think we’re good," Sarah said. "This remote setup is actually working really well. We get so much done without commuting."

"Agreed," Mark added. "Just remember to mute your mic when you’re not speaking, Sarah!"

Sarah laughed. "Noted! No more background noise from the dog."

"Alright, thanks everyone. Have a productive day," Julia said, ending the call.

Julia leaned back in her chair. She realized that remote work required a bit more technical awareness and patience. Things like Wi-Fi connections, screen sharing, and muting microphones were small details, but mastering them made online communication smooth, professional, and surprisingly human.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What was the problem with Sarah’s microphone at the beginning of the call?
2. Why did Julia’s screen sharing fail at first?
3. What feedback did Sarah give about the website layout?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Julia started a... (video call) to review the website layout.
Sarah forgot to... (mute) her microphone, and there was... (background noise) from her dog.
Julia tried to use... (screen sharing), but it didn’t work.
Mark suggested checking the application... (settings / permissions), which fixed the problem.
Sarah liked the colors but said the mobile button was too... (small).
Mark confirmed that the... (loading speed) was much faster now.
Julia promised to fix the button and send the link by... (4 PM).
The team agreed that... (remote work) is effective if you manage technical details well.

Тема: № 55 Конфликты на работе и способы их решения (Workplace Conflicts and Resolution)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. to escalate [ˈeskəleɪt] — обострять, передавать руководству
2. to mediate [ˈmiːdieɪt] — выступать посредником
3. defensive [dɪˈfensɪv] — занимающий оборонительную позицию
4. to address [əˈdres] — решать (проблему), обращаться к
5. to compromise [ˈkɒmprəmaɪz] — идти на компромисс
6. common ground [ˈkɒmən ɡraʊnd] — общая почва, взаимопонимание

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~380 слов.
"Why did you change the color scheme without asking me?" Mark asked, his voice rising. He was the lead developer, and he was clearly frustrated.

Chloe, the graphic designer, crossed her arms. "Because the client hated the blue! I had to fix it before the deadline. Why are you getting so defensive?"

"I’m not defensive," Mark shot back. "But changing the colors means I have to rewrite the CSS code for the entire website. You can't just make unilateral decisions!"

The atmosphere in the open-plan office became very tense. Their manager, David, noticed the argument and walked over. "Okay, let’s take a breath," David said calmly. "We need to address this before it escalates. Let’s go to the meeting room."

Inside, David decided to mediate. "Mark, I understand you’re stressed about the code. Chloe, I understand you wanted to please the client. But if we don't communicate, we create bottlenecks. How can we find common ground here?"

Chloe sighed, her tone softening. "I’m sorry, Mark. I didn’t realize it would take you so long to update the code. I just panicked when the client complained."

Mark rubbed his temples. "I’m sorry too. I shouldn't have yelled. But next time, just ping me on Slack before changing the design. If I know in advance, I can prepare the code."

"What if we set up a quick 10-minute sync every morning?" David suggested. "That way, design and development are always aligned."

"I can agree to that," Mark said. "And I can compromise on the colors if you give me the hex codes by noon tomorrow."

"Deal," Chloe smiled, extending her hand. 

David nodded approvingly. "Great job, guys. Conflicts are normal, but how we resolve them defines our team."

Mark and Chloe left the room feeling relieved. They realized that avoiding conflicts only makes things worse. By talking openly, listening without getting defensive, and finding a practical compromise, they turned a stressful argument into a better workflow.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why was Mark angry at Chloe?
2. How did David help them resolve the conflict?
3. What compromise did they reach at the end?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Mark was angry because Chloe changed the design without asking, which made him rewrite the... (code).
Chloe got... (defensive) and said she did it for the client.
David stepped in to... (mediate) before the argument could... (escalate).
He asked them to find... (common ground) and communicate better.
Chloe apologized and promised to message Mark before making... (changes).
They agreed to a daily sync and to... (compromise) on the workflow.
They learned that resolving conflicts requires open communication and finding a... (compromise).

Тема: № 56 Презентации и публичные выступления (Presentations and Public Speaking)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. stage fright [steɪdʒ fraɪt] — страх сцены, волнение
2. audience [ˈɔːdiəns] — аудитория, слушатели
3. to engage [ɪnˈɡeɪdʒ] — вовлекать, заинтересовывать
4. slide deck [slaɪd dek] — презентация (набор слайдов)
5. eye contact [aɪ ˈkɒntækt] — зрительный контакт
6. to deliver [dɪˈlɪvə] — выступать, произносить (речь)

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~390 слов.
Emma stared at her laptop screen, her hands shaking slightly. "I can't do this, Sam," she told her mentor. "I have to deliver the quarterly report to the board of directors tomorrow, and I have terrible stage fright. What if I forget my lines? What if they ask a question I don't know the answer to?"

Sam smiled gently and pulled up a chair. "First of all, Emma, you know this data better than anyone. You built the financial model. The board doesn't expect a perfect actor; they expect an expert. Let’s work on your slide deck and your delivery."

They opened the presentation. "Rule number one," Sam said. "Don't read from the slides. The slides are just visual aids. Use bullet points and tell a story. How can you engage the audience right from the start?"

"Maybe I could start with a surprising fact?" Emma suggested. "Like, 'Did you know we saved 20% on logistics this quarter?'"

"Perfect!" Sam praised. "That hooks them immediately. Now, what about your body language? You tend to look at the floor when you're nervous."

"I know," Emma admitted. "I'm afraid to look them in the eyes."

"Try the 'lighthouse' technique," Sam advised. "Make brief eye contact with one person, then move to another. It makes everyone feel like you're speaking directly to them. And remember to breathe. If you lose your train of thought, just pause. A three-second silence feels like an eternity to you, but to the audience, it just looks like you're making a thoughtful point."

Emma practiced in the empty conference room for two hours. Sam gave her feedback on her posture and tone. By the end, she felt much more confident.

The next morning, Emma stood in front of the boardroom. She took a deep breath, made eye contact with the CEO, and started her presentation. She didn't read the slides. She told the story of the company's success, using the data to back it up. When the CFO asked a tough question, she didn't panic. She paused, thought for a second, and answered clearly.

After the meeting, the CEO walked up to her. "Excellent presentation, Emma. Very engaging and clear."

Emma texted Sam immediately: "I survived! The lighthouse technique actually worked!" She realized that public speaking isn't about being fearless; it's about preparing well and connecting with your audience.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why was Emma so nervous before the presentation?
2. What did Sam advise her to do with her slide deck?
3. What is the "lighthouse" technique?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Emma had terrible... (stage fright) about presenting to the board.
Her mentor Sam told her not to read from the... (slide deck).
Sam advised her to tell a story to... (engage) the audience.
To fix her body language, Sam taught her the... (lighthouse) technique for making eye contact.
He also told her that a short pause is better than... (panicking).
The next day, Emma delivered a great presentation and answered a tough question from the... (CFO).
The CEO praised her, and Emma realized that public speaking is about... (preparation / connecting).

Тема: № 57 Переговоры и искусство убеждения (Negotiations and the Art of Persuasion)
Уровень: B1-B2 (Pre-Intermediate / Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. leverage [ˈliːvərɪdʒ] — рычаг давления, преимущество
2. concession [kənˈseʃn] — уступка
3. bottom line [ˈbɒtəm laɪn] — итог, конечная цель / минимальная цена
4. to counter-offer [ˈkaʊntər ˈɒfə] — делать встречное предложение
5. mutually beneficial [ˌmjuːtʃuəli ˌbenɪˈfɪʃl] — взаимовыгодный
6. to persuade [pəˈsweɪd] — убеждать

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~410 слов.
Alex sat across the table from Mr. Vance, the supplier. Alex needed to buy 5,000 custom microchips for his tech startup, but the price was too high. 

"Mr. Vance, $10 per unit is above our budget," Alex said calmly. "We were hoping for something closer to $7."

Mr. Vance shook his head. "I understand, Alex, but the cost of raw materials has gone up. $10 is our bottom line for this quality. I can't go lower."

Alex knew he needed leverage to persuade him. "I appreciate the quality, Mr. Vance. But we are also talking to a supplier in Vietnam who quoted us $6.50. We prefer to work with you because of your fast shipping, but we need a competitive price to make it work."

Mr. Vance leaned back, thinking. He didn't want to lose a large order. "What if we meet halfway? I can offer a concession and drop it to $8.50, but only if you sign a two-year contract instead of a one-year."

Alex considered the counter-offer. $8.50 was better, but he wanted to protect his cash flow. "A two-year contract is a big commitment for a startup," Alex replied. "How about this: We sign a two-year contract at $8.00 per unit. In return, we will make you our exclusive supplier for this specific chip. That guarantees you steady business and gives us the price we need."

Mr. Vance smiled slightly. He liked the idea of an exclusive partnership. "You drive a hard bargain, Alex. But I think this is a mutually beneficial agreement. $8.00 it is."

They shook hands and signed the paperwork. 

Walking back to his office, Alex felt a rush of adrenaline. He realized that successful negotiation wasn't about arguing or forcing the other side to lose. It was about understanding their needs, using your leverage politely, and finding a creative solution. By offering exclusivity, he gave Mr. Vance something valuable in exchange for the discount. It was the art of persuasion in action: making the other person feel like they won, too.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why couldn't Mr. Vance accept Alex's initial offer of $7?
2. What leverage did Alex use during the negotiation?
3. What was the final mutually beneficial agreement?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Alex tried to negotiate the price of microchips, but $10 was above his... (budget).
Mr. Vance said $10 was his... (bottom line) because of raw material costs.
Alex used... (leverage) by mentioning a cheaper supplier in Vietnam.
Mr. Vance made a... (concession) and offered $8.50 for a two-year contract.
Alex made a... (counter-offer): $8.00 in exchange for an exclusive partnership.
Mr. Vance agreed because it was a... (mutually beneficial) deal.
Alex realized that negotiation is about understanding needs and the art of... (persuasion).

Тема: № 58 Корпоративный этикет и деловой стиль общения (Corporate Etiquette and Business Communication)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. formal tone [ˈfɔːml təʊn] — формальный тон
2. to phrase [freɪz] — формулировать, выражать
3. boundary [ˈbaʊndri] — граница
4. to cc (carbon copy) [ˌsiː ˈsiː] — ставить в копию
5. to demand [dɪˈmɑːnd] — требовать
6. to follow up [ˈfɒləʊ ʌp] — напоминать, следить за выполнением

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~390 слов.
"Hey, Sarah, can you check this email before I send it to the new client?" asked Leo, a junior account manager. 

Sarah, the senior manager, read the draft on Leo’s screen. It said: *'Hey John, I need the signed contract by tomorrow or we can't start the project. Send it ASAP. Thanks.'*

Sarah raised her eyebrows. "Leo, this is way too casual. And a bit aggressive. You need to adjust your formal tone. We are talking to a corporate client, not a buddy from college."

Leo looked confused. "But we had a great, friendly meeting yesterday! I thought we built a good relationship."

"You did," Sarah explained. "But in written business communication, especially regarding contracts and deadlines, you must maintain professional boundaries. Let’s rephrase this. Instead of saying 'I need' or 'Send it ASAP', try asking politely. How would you say it?"

Leo thought for a moment. "Could you please send the signed contract by tomorrow?"

"Much better," Sarah smiled. "Now, let's add the 'why'. Explain that it's for their benefit. For example: 'To ensure we can launch the project on schedule, could you please return the signed contract by tomorrow at 5 PM?'"

"That sounds much more professional," Leo admitted. "What if he doesn't reply?"

"Then you follow up the next morning with a gentle reminder," Sarah said. "And one more thing: always cc your manager on the first few emails with a new client, just to keep everyone in the loop."

Leo rewrote the email, checked it for typos, and added Sarah to the cc line. *'Dear John, Thank you again for the productive meeting yesterday. To ensure we can launch the project on schedule, could you please return the signed contract by tomorrow at 5 PM? Let me know if you have any questions. Best regards, Leo.'*

He hit send. Two hours later, John replied: *'Hi Leo, attached is the signed contract. Looking forward to starting!'*

Leo showed the reply to Sarah. "It worked! And he didn't even seem annoyed by the deadline."

"Because you didn't demand it, you requested it politely," Sarah said. "Good business etiquette is about making the other person feel respected while still achieving your goals."

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why did Sarah stop Leo from sending his first email draft?
2. How did Sarah suggest Leo rephrase the demand for the contract?
3. Why did Sarah tell Leo to cc her on the email?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Leo wrote an email that was too casual and aggressive, so Sarah told him to adjust his... (formal tone).
She explained that he needs to maintain professional... (boundaries) in written communication.
Instead of... (demanding) the contract ASAP, Sarah told him to... (phrase) it as a polite request.
She also advised him to explain the 'why' and to... (cc) her on the email.
Leo rewrote the email politely and sent it.
The client replied quickly with the signed... (contract).
Leo learned that good etiquette is about making people feel... (respected) while achieving your goals.

Тема: № 59 Командная работа и взаимодействие в коллективе (Teamwork and Collaboration)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. bottleneck [ˈbɒtlnek] — узкое место, препятствие
2. to align [əˈlaɪn] — согласовывать, приводить к единому мнению
3. to step in [step ɪn] — вмешиваться, помогать
4. shared responsibility [ʃeəd rɪˌspɒnsəˈbɪləti] — общая ответственность
5. to collaborate [kəˈlæbəreɪt] — сотрудничать
6. to brainstorm [ˈbreɪnstɔːm] — генерировать идеи, штурмить

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~400 слов.
"We have a major bottleneck," said Maya, the project lead, looking at the timeline on the whiteboard. "The app launch is in three days, but the payment integration isn't working. Dev team, what’s the hold-up?"

Tom, the lead developer, sighed. "We are waiting for the final API keys from the design and product team. We can't finish the code without them."

Lisa, the product manager, frowned. "I sent the keys to your email three days ago, Tom! Didn't you check?"

"I check my email, but I get 200 messages a day!" Tom argued. "You should have pinged me on Slack or handed them to me in the stand-up meeting."

The room grew quiet. The team was misaligned, and the deadline was approaching. Maya knew she had to step in before the blame game ruined the project.

"Okay, team, let's stop pointing fingers," Maya said calmly. "This is a shared responsibility. If the integration fails, we all fail. Let's brainstorm a solution right now. How do we fix this in the next 24 hours?"

Tom rubbed his neck. "If Lisa can sit with me for the next two hours and help me test the API directly, we can bypass the email delay and fix the bugs immediately."

"I can do that," Lisa agreed, closing her laptop. "I'll clear my afternoon schedule. I should have followed up with you to make sure you received the keys. That's my mistake."

"Thanks, Lisa," Tom said, his tone softening. "And I should have raised a red flag yesterday when I couldn't find the email. I assumed you were still working on it."

"Great," Maya smiled. "This is what true collaboration looks like. We don't just do our own tasks in isolation; we communicate to ensure the whole system works."

Tom and Lisa moved to a desk together and worked side-by-side for the rest of the day. By 6 PM, the payment integration was fully functional and tested. 

During the successful app launch three days later, Maya raised a glass of champagne. "To a team that knows how to collaborate, communicate, and overcome bottlenecks together!" 

Everyone cheered. They learned that a successful team isn't one that never makes mistakes, but one that aligns quickly and takes shared responsibility to fix them.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письненно):
1. What was the bottleneck delaying the app launch?
2. Why were Tom and Lisa arguing?
3. How did Maya help the team resolve the issue?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Maya announced a... (bottleneck) because the payment integration wasn't working.
Tom blamed Lisa for not sending the API keys, but she said she emailed them... (three days ago).
Maya stepped in and reminded them it was a... (shared responsibility).
They decided to... (collaborate) directly, with Lisa sitting next to Tom to test the API.
Lisa admitted she should have... (followed up), and Tom admitted he should have spoken up earlier.
By working together, they fixed the bugs and... (launched) the app on time.
They learned that teamwork requires communication and taking... (shared responsibility).
