Тема: № 60 Клиентский сервис и общение с клиентами (Customer Service and Client Communication)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. to handle a complaint [ˈhændl ə kəmˈpleɪnt] — работать с жалобой
2. empathetic [ˌempəˈθetɪk] — сочувствующий, эмпатичный
3. to resolve [rɪˈzɒlv] — решать, улаживать
4. refund [ˈriːfʌnd] — возврат средств
5. to escalate [ˈeskəleɪt] — передавать на уровень выше
6. customer retention [ˈkʌstəmər rɪˈtenʃn] — удержание клиентов

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~410 слов.
The phone rang, and Mia put on her headset. "Thank you for calling TechSupport, my name is Mia. How can I help you today?"

"Help me?" a man’s voice shouted. "I ordered a premium laptop for my business trip, and it never arrived! My flight is tomorrow morning, and I have no computer! This is unacceptable!"

Mia took a deep breath. She knew how to handle a complaint. The first rule was to be empathetic, not defensive. 

"I am so sorry to hear that, sir," Mia said in a calm, soothing voice. "I completely understand how stressful it is to not have your laptop before a business trip. Let me check your order right away. May I have your order number?"

The man, Mr. Davis, gave her the number, still sounding annoyed. Mia checked the system. "Mr. Davis, I see the issue. The courier marked the package as 'delivered' yesterday, but it seems it was left at the wrong address on your street."

"So it's gone? I need a refund immediately!" he demanded.

Mia knew that simply giving a refund would lose the customer forever. Customer retention was about going the extra mile. "Mr. Davis, I don't want you to travel without a computer. I am going to escalate this to our priority shipping team. We will overnight a brand-new laptop to your hotel in New York. It will be there by 8 AM tomorrow, completely free of charge. And I will issue a 20% refund to your card for the terrible inconvenience."

There was a long silence on the line. "Wait, really? You can get it to my hotel by tomorrow morning?"

"Yes, sir," Mia smiled. "I am processing the overnight shipping right now. You will get a tracking number in five minutes."

"Mia, you are a lifesaver," Mr. Davis said, his voice completely changed. "I was so angry, but you completely resolved this. Thank you. I will definitely buy from your company again."

"Have a wonderful and successful business trip, Mr. Davis," Mia replied, ending the call. 

Her manager, who had been listening, gave her a thumbs-up. "Excellent job, Mia. You turned an angry client into a loyal one." Mia realized that great customer service isn't just about following rules; it's about listening with empathy and finding creative solutions to resolve the client's real problem.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why was Mr. Davis angry when he called customer service?
2. What was the actual problem with the delivery?
3. How did Mia resolve the situation to keep the customer happy?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Mr. Davis called to... (handle a complaint / complain) because his laptop didn't arrive before his trip.
Mia was very... (empathetic) and calmly checked his order.
She found out the courier delivered it to the... (wrong address).
Mr. Davis demanded a... (refund), but Mia wanted to focus on customer retention.
She decided to... (escalate) the issue and overnight a new laptop to his hotel for free.
She also offered a 20%... (refund) for the inconvenience.
Mr. Davis was amazed and promised to buy from them again.
Mia learned that great service is about listening and finding creative ways to... (resolve) problems.

Тема: № 61 Рабочие процессы и постановка задач (Workflows and Task Setting)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. workflow [ˈwɜːkfləʊ] — рабочий процесс
2. to assign [əˈsaɪn] — назначать (задачу)
3. deadline [ˈdedlaɪn] — крайний срок
4. milestone [ˈmaɪlstəʊn] — важный этап, веха
5. to track [træk] — отслеживать
6. bottleneck [ˈbɒtlnek] — узкое место, препятствие

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~420 слов.
"Okay, team, let’s look at our workflow for the new website launch," said Alex, the project manager, sharing his screen during the morning meeting. 

Sarah, a new junior developer, raised her hand. "Alex, I’m a bit confused. How do we know which tasks are the most urgent?"

"Great question, Sarah," Alex smiled. "We use our project management tool to assign tasks clearly. Every task has an owner, a priority level, and a strict deadline. For example, I just assigned the homepage design to you, Sarah. The deadline is this Friday."

"Got it," Sarah said, taking notes. "But what if I get stuck? I don’t want to become a bottleneck for the whole team."

"That’s exactly why we have milestones," Alex explained. "A milestone is a checkpoint. For the homepage, your first milestone is the wireframe, due on Wednesday. If you track your progress and see you’re falling behind, you ping me immediately. We don’t wait until Friday to discover a problem."

Mark, the senior designer, chimed in. "Sarah, if you’re stuck on the wireframe, just slide into my DMs. I can review it in ten minutes. That’s how we keep the workflow smooth."

"Thanks, Mark! That makes me feel much better," Sarah replied. 

"Another important rule," Alex added, "is to break big tasks into smaller, manageable pieces. Don’t just write 'Build website' on your to-do list. Break it down: 'Create header', 'Write hero text', 'Add contact form'. It makes tracking progress much easier and less overwhelming."

Sarah nodded, opening the project board on her laptop. "I see it now. The tasks are color-coded, and I can see exactly what Mark is working on, too."

"Exactly," Alex said. "Transparency is key. If everyone knows what everyone else is doing, we avoid duplicate work and missed deadlines. So, Sarah, your first action item is to create the wireframe draft by Wednesday. Can you commit to that?"

"Yes, absolutely," Sarah said confidently. "I’ll set a reminder for Tuesday afternoon to review it with Mark."

"Perfect," Alex concluded. "Let’s keep the communication open, track our milestones, and support each other. Any other questions about the workflow?"

The team shook their heads, ready to start. Sarah realized that a good workflow wasn’t about micromanagement. It was about clear communication, realistic deadlines, and knowing exactly when and how to ask for help.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why was Sarah confused at the beginning of the meeting?
2. What is a "milestone" according to Alex?
3. How did Mark offer to help Sarah?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Alex explained the team's... (workflow) for the website launch.
He showed Sarah how they... (assign) tasks with a clear priority and... (deadline).
Sarah was worried about becoming a... (bottleneck) if she got stuck.
Alex explained that they use... (milestones) as checkpoints to... (track) progress.
Mark offered to review Sarah's wireframe quickly to keep the workflow... (smooth).
Alex advised breaking big tasks into smaller pieces to avoid feeling... (overwhelmed).
Sarah realized that a good workflow is about clear communication and knowing when to ask for... (help).

Тема: № 62 Стресс на работе и work-life balance (Workplace Stress and Work-Life Balance)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. burnout [ˈbɜːnaʊt] — эмоциональное выгорание
2. to unwind [ʌnˈwaɪnd] — расслабляться, отдыхать
3. boundaries [ˈbaʊndəriz] — личные границы
4. overtime [ˈəʊvətaɪm] — сверхурочная работа
5. to prioritize [praɪˈɒrɪtaɪz] — расставлять приоритеты
6. mental health [ˈmentl helθ] — психическое здоровье

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~430 слов.
It was 7:30 PM on a Thursday, and the office was almost empty. Emma was still at her desk, staring at her screen with tired eyes. Her phone buzzed with another email notification.

"Emma, are you still here?" asked Mark, the team lead, as he walked by with his coat on. 

"Yeah, just finishing up the quarterly report," Emma sighed, rubbing her temples. "I have to send it to the director by tomorrow morning."

Mark pulled up a chair. "Emma, you’ve been working overtime every day this week. You look exhausted. Are you okay?"

"I’m fine, just a bit stressed," Emma replied, though her voice sounded shaky. "If I don’t finish this, the whole project will be delayed."

"The project will be fine," Mark said gently. "But your mental health won’t be if you keep pushing yourself like this. Burnout is real, and I don’t want to see you get there."

Emma looked down. "I just feel like if I don’t do everything perfectly, I’ll let the team down."

"Listen to me," Mark said firmly but kindly. "You are a valuable member of this team, but you are not a machine. You need to set boundaries. Do you check your work email after 8 PM?"

"Usually, yes," Emma admitted. "I feel like I have to be available."

"That’s a bad habit," Mark said. "You need time to unwind. When you go home, close your laptop, put your phone on silent, and do something you enjoy. Read a book, take a walk, cook dinner. Anything but work."

"But what about the report?" Emma asked.

"Let’s prioritize," Mark suggested. "What is absolutely essential for tomorrow? Just the executive summary and the main data charts. The rest can wait until Monday. I will talk to the director and explain that we are delivering the core data first."

Emma felt a huge wave of relief. "Really? You would do that?"

"Of course. We support each other here," Mark smiled. "Now, pack your things. Go home, get a good night’s sleep, and come back fresh tomorrow."

"Thank you, Mark. I really needed to hear that," Emma said, shutting down her computer. 

That evening, Emma didn’t check her email once. She cooked a nice dinner, watched a movie, and went to bed early. The next day, she arrived at the office feeling refreshed and focused. She realized that work-life balance wasn’t a luxury; it was a necessity for long-term success and happiness.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why was Emma still at the office at 7:30 PM?
2. What bad habit did Emma admit to having?
3. How did Mark help Emma prioritize her work?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Emma was working late and felt... (stressed) about finishing the report.
Mark noticed she was working... (overtime) every day and warned her about... (burnout).
He told her it was important to set... (boundaries) and protect her... (mental health).
Emma admitted she always checked her email after hours instead of taking time to... (unwind).
Mark helped her... (prioritize) the report, saying only the core data was needed for tomorrow.
He told her to go home, rest, and come back... (fresh).
Emma realized that work-life balance is a... (necessity) for long-term success.

Тема: № 63 Карьерный рост и профессиональное развитие (Career Growth and Professional Development)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. mentorship [ˈmentɔːʃɪp] — наставничество
2. to upskill [ʌpˈskɪl] — повышать квалификацию
3. long-term goal [lɒŋ tɜːm ɡəʊl] — долгосрочная цель
4. constructive feedback [kənˈstrʌktɪv ˈfiːdbæk] — конструктивная обратная связь
5. networking [ˈnetwɜːkɪŋ] — нетворкинг, установление связей
6. to advance [ədˈvɑːns] — продвигаться (по карьерной лестнице)

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~440 слов.
"David, you’ve been a solid marketing specialist for two years," said Lisa, the marketing director, during their one-on-one meeting. "But I know you want to advance to a senior role. Have you thought about your professional development plan?"

David nodded eagerly. "Yes, Lisa. I really want to grow, but I’m not sure what steps to take next. I feel like I’ve hit a plateau."

"That’s completely normal," Lisa reassured him. "Let’s build a roadmap together. First, we need to define your long-term goal. Where do you see yourself in three years?"

"I’d love to lead a marketing team," David replied. "But I lack experience in managing people and advanced data analytics."

"Perfect. That gives us a clear direction," Lisa said. "To upskill in data analytics, I recommend taking the advanced Google Analytics certification course. The company will pay for it. As for leadership, I can offer you mentorship. I’ll let you lead the upcoming product launch campaign."

David’s eyes widened. "Really? Lead the whole campaign?"

"Yes, but with my guidance," Lisa smiled. "You’ll make the decisions, but we will have weekly check-ins. I will give you constructive feedback on your management style, your communication, and your strategic thinking."

"That sounds amazing," David said. "What about networking? I feel like I only talk to people in our immediate department."

"Great point," Lisa said. "Professional development isn’t just about hard skills. I want you to attend the industry conference next month. Talk to other marketing leaders, exchange contacts, and learn about new trends. Bring back three key insights to share with our team."

"I will definitely do that," David said, writing everything down. "This makes the path to a senior role feel much more achievable."

"Remember, David, career growth doesn’t happen overnight," Lisa added. "It requires continuous learning, stepping out of your comfort zone, and actively seeking feedback. I believe in your potential."

Six months later, David successfully led the product launch. The campaign exceeded all targets, and his team praised his clear communication and supportive leadership. 

During his next performance review, Lisa smiled. "You’ve proven you’re ready, David. Congratulations on your promotion to Senior Marketing Manager."

David realized that professional development wasn’t a mystery. It was a deliberate process of setting goals, upskilling, finding a good mentor, and embracing new challenges.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What was David’s long-term goal?
2. How did Lisa offer to help David improve his leadership skills?
3. What did Lisa ask David to do at the industry conference?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Lisa and David discussed his desire to... (advance) to a senior role.
They defined his... (long-term goal) of leading a marketing team.
To upskill, Lisa suggested he get a Google Analytics... (certification).
She also offered... (mentorship) by letting him lead the next campaign.
Lisa promised to give him... (constructive feedback) during weekly check-ins.
She also encouraged him to do some... (networking) at an industry conference.
Six months later, David succeeded and got his... (promotion).
He learned that career growth requires continuous learning and seeking... (feedback).

Тема: № 64 Личные финансы и планирование бюджета (Personal Finance and Budget Planning)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. budget [ˈbʌdʒɪt] — бюджет
2. expenses [ɪkˈspensɪz] — расходы
3. to save up [seɪv ʌp] — копить (на что-либо)
4. emergency fund [ɪˈmɜːdʒənsi fʌnd] — резервный фонд, подушка безопасности
5. to track spending [træk ˈspendɪŋ] — отслеживать траты
6. financial goal [faɪˈnænʃl ɡəʊl] — финансовая цель

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~430 слов.
"Tom, we need to talk about our money," Anna said, sitting at the kitchen table with a stack of bank statements. "We keep saying we want to buy a house, but our savings account hasn’t grown in months."

Tom sighed, running a hand through his hair. "I know, Anna. I feel like we earn a decent salary, but the money just disappears. I don’t even know where it goes."

"That’s exactly the problem," Anna said gently. "We don’t have a budget. We need to sit down and track our spending for the last three months."

They opened their banking app and started categorizing their expenses. Rent, utilities, and groceries were normal. But then they looked at the "Entertainment" and "Dining Out" categories.

"Wow," Tom said, his eyes wide. "We spent $600 on restaurants and coffee shops last month alone? And $200 on subscription services we barely use?"

"Exactly," Anna nodded. "If we want to reach our financial goal of saving for a house down payment, we have to make changes. We need to cut unnecessary expenses."

"Okay, you’re right," Tom admitted. "What’s the plan?"

"First," Anna said, "we create a strict monthly budget. We allocate a specific amount for groceries, a small amount for fun, and we automate a transfer to our savings account on payday."

"What about an emergency fund?" Tom asked. "My car is getting old, and I’m worried about unexpected repairs."

"Great point," Anna smiled. "We will build an emergency fund first. Our goal is to save three months' worth of living expenses. Once we have that safety net, we can focus entirely on the house down payment."

"This sounds like a lot of discipline," Tom said. "No more Friday night pizza?"

"We can still have Friday night pizza," Anna laughed. "But we’ll buy the ingredients and make it at home. It’s cheaper and more fun. We just need to be mindful of our choices."

Tom agreed. They set up a budgeting app on their phones to track spending in real-time. For the first two weeks, it was challenging. Tom really wanted to buy a new video game, and Anna missed her daily fancy coffee. But they stuck to the plan.

By the end of the third month, they checked their savings account. They had saved $1,500 more than usual. 

"Look at this!" Tom cheered. "We actually did it."

Anna hugged him. "We did. And it feels great to be in control of our money." They realized that personal finance wasn’t about restricting themselves; it was about making intentional choices to build a secure future.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why were Tom and Anna worried about their finances?
2. What surprising expense did they discover when tracking their spending?
3. What was their first financial priority before saving for a house?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Anna and Tom realized they weren't reaching their... (financial goal) of buying a house.
They decided to sit down and... (track spending) for the last three months.
They were shocked by their high... (expenses) on restaurants and subscriptions.
Anna suggested creating a strict monthly... (budget) and automating savings.
Tom wisely pointed out the need for an... (emergency fund) for unexpected costs.
They agreed to cut unnecessary costs, like making pizza at home to... (save up).
After three months, they successfully saved $1,500 more than usual.
They learned that managing money is about making intentional... (choices).

Тема: № 65 Технологии и гаджеты в повседневной жизни (Technology and Gadgets in Everyday Life)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. smart home [smɑːt həʊm] — умный дом
2. wearable device [ˈweərəbl dɪˈvaɪs] — носимое устройство (например, смарт-часы)
3. to sync [sɪŋk] — синхронизировать
4. battery life [ˈbætəri laɪf] — время работы батареи
5. notification [ˌnəʊtɪfɪˈkeɪʃn] — уведомление
6. user-friendly [ˈjuːzə ˈfrendli] — удобный в использовании

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~420 слов.
"Dad, stop tapping the screen so hard, it’s not going to make it faster," Lily laughed, watching her father struggle with his new smartwatch.

John frowned. "I just don’t understand why it’s not showing my messages. I thought this was supposed to be a user-friendly device."

"It is, Dad, you just haven’t set it up correctly," Lily said, taking the watch from him. "First, we need to sync it with your phone via Bluetooth. Have you downloaded the companion app?"

"I think so," John said, pulling out his smartphone. "But my phone is already buzzing like crazy. I’m getting notifications for everything: emails, news, the weather, my friend’s cat photos. It’s overwhelming."

Lily nodded. "That’s a common problem with wearable devices. You need to customize your notification settings. You don’t need to know the weather every ten minutes. Let’s turn off everything except calls and text messages from family."

She tapped on the screen for a minute. "There. Much better. Now, let’s talk about battery life. You need to charge this every night, just like your phone. If you forget, it will die by noon tomorrow."

"Every night? That’s annoying," John grumbled.

"It’s a small price to pay for the benefits," Lily argued. "Think about it: it tracks your steps, monitors your heart rate, and lets you answer calls without digging your phone out of your pocket. Plus, we can connect it to your smart home system."

"Smart home? You mean the lights and the thermostat?" John asked, intrigued.

"Exactly," Lily smiled. "Once it’s all connected, you can say, 'Turn off the living room lights,' or your watch can automatically lower the thermostat when you leave for work. It makes daily life so much more convenient."

John’s eyes lit up. "Okay, that actually sounds useful. Show me how to do that."

For the next hour, Lily patiently guided him through the setup process. They connected the watch, adjusted the notifications, and linked the smart home hub. 

"Try it now," Lily said. "Say, 'Turn on the kitchen lights'."

John cleared his throat. "Turn on the kitchen lights." Instantly, the lights in the next room flickered on. 

"Wow," John said, genuinely impressed. "That’s like magic."

"It’s just technology, Dad," Lily grinned. "But yes, it’s pretty cool. Just remember to charge it tonight!"

John looked at his wrist, feeling like he had stepped into the future. He realized that while technology could be confusing at first, taking the time to learn it could make everyday life significantly easier and more enjoyable.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):

2. How did Lily solve the problem of too many notifications?
3. What smart home feature excited John the most?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):

Lily explained that he needed to... (sync) it with his phone.
John complained about too many... (notifications), so Lily helped him customize the settings.
Lily also reminded him to charge it every night to maintain good... (battery life).
She explained that the watch could connect to his... (smart home) system.
John tested it by asking the watch to turn on the kitchen... (lights).

Тема: № 66 Путешествия и командировки (Travel and Business Trips)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. business trip [ˈbɪznəs trɪp] — командировка
2. itinerary [aɪˈtɪnərəri] — маршрут, план поездки
3. expense report [ɪkˈspens rɪˈpɔːt] — отчет о расходах
4. jet lag [dʒet læɡ] — смена часовых поясов, джетлаг
5. to book [bʊk] — бронировать
6. accommodation [əˌkɒməˈdeɪʃn] — размещение, жилье

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~430 слов.
"Maria, you look like you’re about to conquer the world," David said, noticing her organized desk and color-coded planner. 

"I’m trying to," Maria replied, taking a deep breath. "I have my first international business trip to London next week, and I’m making sure I don’t forget anything."

David, who traveled for work frequently, smiled. "London is great. But it can be overwhelming if you’re not prepared. Have you booked your accommodation yet?"

"Yes, the company booked a hotel near the client’s office," Maria said. "But I’m struggling with the itinerary. I have three meetings on Monday, a dinner on Tuesday, and a flight back on Wednesday evening. I’m worried about jet lag."

"Ah, jet lag," David nodded. "My best advice: as soon as you get on the plane, set your watch to London time. Try to sleep on the flight if it’s nighttime there. When you arrive, force yourself to stay awake until at least 9 PM local time. Get some sunlight and walk around. It resets your internal clock."

"That’s a great tip, I’ll write that down," Maria said, grabbing her pen. "What about meals? Should I book restaurants in advance?"

"For the client dinner, yes, absolutely," David advised. "But for your own meals, keep it flexible. Also, keep all your receipts. Coffee, taxi rides, meals. You will need them for your expense report when you get back."

Maria groaned. "I hate doing expense reports. It takes forever."

"I know, it’s tedious," David agreed. "But if you take a photo of every receipt immediately and upload it to the company app, it takes five minutes at the end of the trip. Don’t leave it until the last day, or you’ll lose them."

"Smart," Maria said. "Is there anything else I should know?"

"Just one thing," David said seriously. "Leave some buffer time in your itinerary. London traffic can be unpredictable, and the Tube can get delayed. If you have a meeting at 10 AM, plan to leave the hotel by 8:30 AM. It’s better to be early and have a coffee than to be late and stressed."

"Buffer time. Got it," Maria smiled, feeling much more confident. "Thank you, David. I feel like I actually know what I’m doing now."

"Have a fantastic trip, Maria," David said. "And bring me back some proper British tea."

"I will!" she laughed. Maria realized that a successful business trip wasn’t just about the meetings. It was about careful planning, managing your energy, and handling the small details like receipts and travel time.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):

2. What was David’s advice for dealing with jet lag?
3. Why is it important to keep all receipts during a business trip?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):

She had already... (booked) her accommodation near the client's office.

David reminded her to save all receipts for the... (expense report).
He suggested taking photos of receipts immediately to save... (time).

Тема: № 67 Эмоции и управление ими (Emotions and Managing Them)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. overwhelming [ˌəʊvəˈwelmɪŋ] — подавляющий, невыносимый
2. to cope with [kəʊp wɪð] — справляться с
3. trigger [ˈtrɪɡə] — триггер, причина эмоциональной реакции
4. to take a deep breath [teɪk ə diːp breθ] — сделать глубокий вдох
5. to express [ɪkˈspres] — выражать
6. to calm down [kɑːm daʊn] — успокаиваться

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~420 слов.
Sarah stared at her computer screen, feeling her heart race. She had just received a very rude email from a client. The tone was aggressive, and it felt like a personal attack. She felt an overwhelming wave of anger and wanted to reply immediately with an equally angry message.

"Whoa, Sarah, you look like you're about to explode," said her colleague, Ben, noticing her tense posture.

"I am," Sarah said, her voice shaking slightly. "This client is completely unreasonable. I want to tell him exactly what I think."

Ben pulled up a chair. "I get it. But replying while you're angry is never a good idea. It's a classic trigger. Why don't you take a deep breath and step away from the keyboard for five minutes?"

Sarah hesitated, but she knew Ben was right. She stood up, walked to the break room, and poured herself a glass of water. She took three deep breaths, focusing only on the air filling her lungs. Slowly, she began to calm down. 

When she returned to her desk, the anger had faded, replaced by a clearer perspective. "Okay, I'm ready," she said. "But how do I cope with this without sounding weak?"

"You don't have to be weak, just professional," Ben advised. "Express your concerns factually. Acknowledge his frustration, but set a clear boundary. For example: 'I understand you are frustrated with the delay. However, I cannot accept this tone. Let's focus on solving the problem.'"

Sarah nodded and started typing. She kept her sentences short and polite. She didn't apologize for things that weren't her fault, but she offered a concrete solution to fix the issue. She read it twice, deleted one emotional adjective, and hit send.

An hour later, the client replied. "Thank you for the quick response. I apologize for my earlier tone. I was stressed about the deadline. Your solution works for me."

Sarah let out a long sigh of relief. "I can't believe it worked."

"Emotions are normal, Sarah," Ben smiled. "But managing them is a skill. You didn't let your anger control you. You controlled the situation."

Sarah realized that emotional intelligence wasn't about never feeling angry or stressed. It was about recognizing the trigger, pausing to calm down, and choosing a constructive way to express her feelings.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why did Sarah feel an overwhelming wave of anger?
2. What advice did Ben give Sarah when she wanted to reply angrily?
3. How did the client react to Sarah’s professional response?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Sarah received a rude email that acted as an emotional... (trigger).
She wanted to reply angrily, but Ben told her to step away and... (take a deep breath).
After a few minutes, she began to... (calm down) and see the situation clearly.
Ben advised her to... (express) her concerns factually and professionally.
Sarah wrote a polite email, offered a solution, and avoided... (emotional) language.
The client apologized for his tone and accepted her... (solution).
Sarah learned that managing emotions is about pausing and choosing a... (constructive) response.

Тема: № 68 Привычки и самодисциплина (Habits and Self-Discipline)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. habit [ˈhæbɪt] — привычка
2. willpower [ˈwɪlpaʊə] — сила воли
3. consistent [kənˈsɪstənt] — последовательный, постоянный
4. to stick to [stɪk tuː] — придерживаться
5. routine [ruːˈtiːn] — распорядок, рутина
6. to break a habit [breɪk ə ˈhæbɪt] — избавиться от привычки

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~430 слов.
"I give up," Mark groaned, throwing his running shoes into the corner of his room. "I’ve tried to wake up at 6 AM to exercise for a week, and I failed every single day. I just don’t have enough willpower."

His sister, Lisa, laughed gently. "Mark, willpower is overrated. You can’t build a new habit just by forcing yourself. You need a better system."

"What do you mean?" Mark asked, sitting on the bed. 

"Right now, your goal is too big," Lisa explained. "Waking up at 6 AM and running for an hour is a huge change. Your brain resists it. Instead, try the 'two-minute rule'. Make the habit so small that it’s impossible to say no."

"Like what?" 

"Just put on your running shoes and step outside. That’s it. You don’t even have to run. Just do it for two minutes. Once you’re outside, you might decide to run, but the goal is just to step out."

Mark was skeptical but decided to try. The next morning, his alarm went off at 6 AM. He felt the familiar urge to hit snooze. But he remembered the rule. He sat up, put on his shoes, and walked to the front door. 

"Okay, I did it," he muttered. But since he was already outside in the cool morning air, he decided to jog for just five minutes. 

He did this for a week. Sometimes he ran for five minutes, sometimes for twenty. The key was that he was consistent. He didn't focus on the distance; he focused on sticking to the routine.

A month later, Lisa found him tying his shoes at 5:50 AM. "Look at you! You didn't even need the alarm."

"It’s weird," Mark smiled. "It doesn’t feel like a chore anymore. It’s just what I do in the morning. I think I finally broke my habit of hitting the snooze button."

"That’s the magic of consistency," Lisa said. "You didn’t rely on willpower. You built a system that made the right choice the easy choice."

Mark realized that self-discipline wasn’t about punishing yourself or having superhuman strength. It was about starting small, being consistent, and letting the routine do the heavy lifting.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why did Mark want to give up on his morning exercise?
2. What is the "two-minute rule" that Lisa suggested?
3. How did Mark’s morning routine change after a month?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Mark tried to wake up at 6 AM to run but failed due to a lack of... (willpower).
Lisa told him that willpower is overrated and he needed a better... (system).
She suggested the... (two-minute rule): just put on shoes and step outside.
Mark tried it and found that once he was outside, he wanted to... (jog / run).
He focused on being... (consistent) rather than running long distances.
After a month, he was waking up before his... (alarm) went off.
He realized that self-discipline is about starting small and building a... (routine).

Тема: № 69 Креативность и генерация идей (Creativity and Idea Generation)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. brainstorming [ˈbreɪnstɔːmɪŋ] — мозговой штурм
2. out of the box [aʊt əv ðə bɒks] — нестандартно, творчески
3. inspiration [ˌɪnspəˈreɪʃn] — вдохновение
4. to jot down [dʒɒt daʊn] — быстро записывать
5. creative block [kriˈeɪtɪv blɒk] — творческий кризис, ступор
6. perspective [pəˈspektɪv] — точка зрения, перспектива

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~440 слов.
The marketing team sat around the conference table in complete silence. They had a creative block. The deadline for the new eco-friendly product campaign was in two days, and they had zero good ideas.

"Okay, let’s try brainstorming again," said Chloe, the team leader. "No bad ideas. Just shout out whatever comes to mind."

"Green trees?" suggested Tom.
"Recycling bins?" added Sarah.

Chloe sighed. "Guys, these are clichés. We need something out of the box. Something that will make people stop and think."

The room went silent again. The pressure was suffocating. 

"You know what?" Chloe said, standing up. "Staring at this whiteboard isn’t working. Let’s change our perspective. Everyone, grab a notebook and a pen. We are going for a 15-minute walk in the park. No talking about work. Just observe."

The team looked at each other, confused, but they followed her outside. The fresh air felt amazing. They walked past a playground, a busy cafe, and a street musician. 

When they returned to the office, Chloe asked, "What did you notice?"

"I noticed how kids share toys without being asked," Tom said.
"I saw a barista remember a regular customer’s name and make them smile," Sarah added.
"And I saw the street musician using recycled bottles as drums," Mark chimed in.

Chloe’s eyes lit up. "Yes! That’s it! 'Sharing', 'Community', 'Upcycling'. Let’s jot down these concepts. Our campaign shouldn’t just be about the product. It should be about the small, everyday actions that build a sustainable community."

The energy in the room shifted instantly. Ideas started flowing. They decided to create a video campaign showing real people doing small, creative eco-friendly acts, set to the rhythm of recycled instruments. 

"See?" Chloe smiled, looking at the filled whiteboard. "Creativity doesn’t come from forcing it. It comes from stepping away, changing your environment, and looking at the world from a new perspective."

Two days later, the client loved the campaign. It was fresh, authentic, and completely different from anything they had seen before. The team learned that a creative block isn’t a dead end; it’s just a sign that you need to change your approach.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why was the marketing team frustrated at the beginning of the story?
2. What did Chloe suggest the team do to overcome the creative block?
3. What was the final idea for the eco-friendly campaign?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, the team had a... (creative block) and couldn't think of good ideas for the campaign.
Chloe stopped the boring... (brainstorming) and took the team for a walk in the park.
She wanted them to change their... (perspective) and observe the world.
They noticed small acts of kindness and... (recycling / upcycling).
They decided to... (jot down) these concepts and focus on community actions.
The new idea was completely... (out of the box) and authentic.
The client loved the campaign, and the team learned that creativity needs a change of... (environment / approach).

Тема: № 70 Изменения и адаптация к новому (Changes and Adapting to the New)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. to adapt [əˈdæpt] — адаптироваться, приспосабливаться
2. uncertainty [ʌnˈsɜːtnti] — неопределенность
3. resistance [rɪˈzɪstəns] — сопротивление
4. flexible [ˈfleksəbl] — гибкий
5. to embrace [ɪmˈbreɪs] — принимать, охотно соглашаться
6. transition [trænˈzɪʃn] — переход, трансформация

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~430 слов.
"Have you seen the new software update?" Mark asked, shaking his head in disbelief. "They changed the entire interface. It’s going to take weeks to learn this."

His colleague, Anna, nodded. "I know. Everyone in the office is complaining. There is so much resistance to this transition."

"Why do they always change things that already work?" Mark grumbled. "I liked the old version. This new one is confusing."

Anna smiled gently. "Change is always uncomfortable at first. It brings uncertainty. But we have two choices: we can complain and fall behind, or we can be flexible and adapt."

"Easy for you to say," Mark replied. "You learn new things quickly."

"It’s not about being fast," Anna explained. "It’s about your mindset. Instead of seeing this as a problem, try to embrace it. Think about the benefits. The new software automates the reports we used to do manually. That will save us hours every week."

Mark thought about this. "I guess you’re right. The manual reports were a nightmare."

"Exactly," Anna said. "Let’s tackle this together. I watched a 10-minute tutorial video this morning. I can show you the three most important shortcuts. It will make the transition much smoother."

"Okay, I’m in," Mark said, feeling a bit more optimistic. 

Anna pulled her chair next to his desk. For the next twenty minutes, she showed him how to navigate the new dashboard, where to find the automated reports, and how to customize his view. 

"Wow, that’s actually much faster," Mark admitted, clicking through the menus. "I didn’t realize it could do that."

"See?" Anna smiled. "Once you get past the initial shock, it’s a huge upgrade. And if you help others learn it, you’ll become the go-to person in the department."

Over the next week, Mark stopped complaining. He practiced the new shortcuts, asked questions, and even helped two other colleagues who were struggling. By the end of the month, he was using the software more efficiently than ever before.

He realized that resistance to change is a natural reaction, but fighting it only causes stress. By choosing to be flexible and embracing the transition, he turned a frustrating situation into an opportunity for growth.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why was Mark frustrated with the new software update?
2. What two choices did Anna say they had regarding the change?
3. How did Mark’s attitude change by the end of the month?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Mark complained about the new software, noting the high... (resistance) in the office.
Anna explained that change brings... (uncertainty), but we can choose to be... (flexible).
She suggested he try to... (embrace) the change and focus on the benefits, like automated reports.
Anna showed Mark a short tutorial to make the... (transition) smoother.
Mark realized the new system was actually... (faster / more efficient).
He started practicing and even helped his... (colleagues) learn the new software.
He learned that adapting to change is an... (opportunity) for growth, not just a problem.

Тема: № 71 Социальные сети и цифровая коммуникация (Social Media and Digital Communication)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. digital footprint [ˈdɪdʒɪtl ˈfʊtprɪnt] — цифровой след
2. to scroll [skrəʊl] — пролистывать (ленту)
3. comparison [kəmˈpærɪsn] — сравнение
4. authentic [ɔːˈθentɪk] — подлинный, настоящий
5. to disconnect [ˌdɪskəˈnekt] — отключаться (от интернета)
6. screen time [skriːn taɪm] — время, проведенное у экрана

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~420 слов.
Emma lay on her bed, her face illuminated by the blue light of her phone. She had been scrolling through Instagram for an hour. She saw pictures of her friends on exotic vacations, colleagues getting promotions, and influencers with perfect lives. 

A heavy feeling settled in her chest. "Why is everyone else’s life so much better than mine?" she whispered to herself. The constant comparison was making her feel anxious and inadequate.

Her roommate, Chloe, walked into the room and saw Emma’s sad expression. "Still scrolling? You’ve been on that app for hours."

"I know," Emma sighed, putting the phone down. "But I can’t stop. And it makes me feel terrible. I feel like I’m falling behind."

Chloe sat on the edge of the bed. "That’s the trap of social media, Em. People only post their highlight reels, not their real, messy lives. You are comparing your behind-the-scenes to everyone else’s stage."

"But it’s so addictive," Emma admitted. "My screen time report said I spend four hours a day on my phone. It’s crazy."

"Let’s try an experiment," Chloe suggested. "This weekend, let’s do a digital detox. We will disconnect from social media for 48 hours. No scrolling, no checking notifications. Just real life."

"Two days? That sounds terrifying," Emma laughed nervously. 

"Just try it," Chloe smiled. "We can go hiking, cook a big dinner, or just read books. I promise, the world won’t end."

Emma agreed. On Saturday morning, she turned off her phone notifications and put the device in a drawer. At first, she felt a strong urge to check it. Her hand twitched toward the drawer several times. But then, she picked up a novel she had been meaning to read for months. 

By Sunday afternoon, Emma felt a strange sense of calm. She and Chloe went for a long walk in the park, had deep conversations, and baked cookies. There was no pressure to take the perfect photo or get likes. It felt authentic and refreshing.

On Monday morning, Emma checked her phone. She had missed a few memes and a couple of group chat messages, but nothing important. 

"You look different," Chloe noted. "More relaxed."

"I feel different," Emma smiled. "I realized that my digital footprint doesn’t define my worth. Real connection happens offline."

Emma decided to keep her screen time limited to one hour a day. She learned that disconnecting from the digital world was the best way to reconnect with herself and the people around her.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why did Emma feel anxious after using Instagram?
2. What experiment did Chloe suggest to Emma?
3. How did Emma feel after the weekend without social media?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Emma spent hours... (scrolling) through social media and felt bad due to constant... (comparison).
Chloe explained that people only show their highlight reels, not... (real) life.
Emma’s... (screen time) was four hours a day, which she wanted to reduce.
Chloe suggested a 48-hour... (digital detox) to disconnect from the internet.
At first, Emma wanted to check her phone, but she started... (reading) and going for walks instead.
The weekend felt... (authentic) and refreshing without the pressure of social media.
Emma realized that her... (digital footprint) doesn’t define her worth, and real connection happens... (offline).

Тема: № 72 Личные границы и умение говорить «нет» (Personal Boundaries and the Ability to Say "No")
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. boundary [ˈbaʊndri] — граница
2. to say no [seɪ nəʊ] — говорить «нет», отказывать
3. guilt [ɡɪlt] — чувство вины
4. to prioritize [praɪˈɒrɪtaɪz] — расставлять приоритеты
5. assertive [əˈsɜːtɪv] — уверенный в себе, настойчивый (в хорошем смысле)
6. respect [rɪˈspekt] — уважение

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~430 слов.
"Hey, Alex, can you take on the Johnson project? I know you’re busy, but you’re the only one who can handle it," said David, the department manager, dropping a thick folder on Alex’s desk.

Alex’s stomach tightened. He was already working late every day to finish his current tasks. But the word "no" stuck in his throat. He always feared disappointing people. 

"Uh, sure, David. I’ll figure it out," Alex said, forcing a smile. 

David nodded and walked away. Alex immediately felt a wave of guilt and exhaustion. He knew that taking on another project would lead to burnout, but he didn’t know how to set a boundary.

Later that day, Alex talked to his mentor, Sarah. "I just agreed to do the Johnson project, but I’m drowning in work. I can’t say no to my boss."

Sarah listened carefully. "Alex, saying yes to everything is not a sign of dedication. It’s a sign of poor boundaries. If you burn out, you can’t help anyone. You need to learn to be assertive."

"But how do I say no without sounding rude or lazy?" Alex asked.

"It’s not about saying a flat 'no'," Sarah explained. "It’s about prioritizing. You can say: 'David, I would love to help with the Johnson project. However, I am currently fully committed to the Smith project, which is due on Friday. If I take on Johnson, the quality of both will suffer. Can we discuss which one should be my top priority?'"

Alex’s eyes widened. "That sounds so professional. It’s not a refusal; it’s a negotiation."

"Exactly," Sarah smiled. "You are showing respect for your own time and the quality of your work. Good managers respect that."

The next morning, Alex went to David’s office. His heart was pounding, but he took a deep breath and used Sarah’s script. 

David listened, looked at Alex’s current workload, and nodded thoughtfully. "You’re right, Alex. I didn’t realize how full your plate was. Let’s delay the Johnson project and assign it to Mark instead. You focus on the Smith project."

Alex walked out of the office feeling a massive weight lift off his shoulders. He didn’t get fired. He didn’t seem lazy. In fact, David seemed to respect him more for being honest about his capacity.

Alex realized that setting personal boundaries wasn’t selfish. It was essential for his well-being and professional success. By learning to say no politely and prioritize his tasks, he took control of his career and his life.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why did Alex feel guilty after agreeing to the Johnson project?
2. What advice did Sarah give Alex about saying no?
3. How did David react when Alex explained his workload?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, David asked Alex to take on a new project, and Alex agreed despite being... (busy).
Alex felt... (guilt) and exhaustion because he couldn't set a... (boundary).
His mentor Sarah told him that saying yes to everything leads to... (burnout).
She taught him to be... (assertive) and explain his current commitments.
Alex learned to... (prioritize) his tasks and negotiate instead of just refusing.
He spoke to David professionally, and David agreed to give the project to... (Mark).
Alex realized that setting boundaries is essential for... (well-being) and earns... (respect) from others.

Тема: № 73 Совещания и эффективное проведение встреч (Meetings and Effective Meeting Facilitation)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. facilitator [fəˈsɪlɪteɪtə] — ведущий, модератор встречи
2. timekeeper [ˈtaɪmkiːpə] — хранитель времени
3. to go off-topic [ɡəʊ ɒf ˈtɒpɪk] — отклоняться от темы
4. action items [ˈækʃən ˈaɪtəmz] — конкретные задачи, действия по итогам встречи
5. to wrap up [ræp ʌp] — завершать, подводить итоги
6. consensus [kənˈsensəs] — консенсус, общее согласие

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~430 слов.
The weekly marketing meeting was turning into a disaster. It had been going on for an hour, and they were still discussing the color of the new logo. 

"I just think blue is more trustworthy," Mark argued for the fifth time.
"But our competitor uses blue!" Lisa countered. 

The team leader, David, rubbed his temples. The meeting was completely derailed. He realized he needed to step up as a proper facilitator. 

"Okay, everyone, let’s pause," David said, raising his hand. "We are going off-topic. The agenda says we have ten minutes left, and we still need to approve the Q3 budget. Let’s make a decision on the logo right now to reach a consensus, or we table it for next week."

"Let’s table it," Mark sighed. "We can’t agree today."

"Agreed," David said. "Now, Lisa, you are our timekeeper today. Please give us a two-minute warning before the hour is up."

"Got it," Lisa said, setting a timer on her phone. 

David quickly guided the team through the budget slides. Because he kept the discussion focused and prevented side conversations, they reviewed all the numbers in fifteen minutes. 

"Great job, team," David said, looking at the clock. "We have two minutes left. Let’s wrap up. What are our action items?"

Lisa read from her notes: "Mark will research three alternative logo colors by Friday. I will finalize the Q3 budget spreadsheet by tomorrow noon. And David will send the meeting minutes to the director."

"Perfect," David smiled. "Meeting adjourned. That was much more efficient than last week."

As the team packed up their laptops, Mark turned to David. "Thanks for stepping in, David. I felt like we were going to talk about logos until midnight."

"That’s why we need a strong facilitator," David replied. "A good meeting isn’t about talking the most; it’s about respecting everyone’s time, sticking to the agenda, and leaving with clear action items. We’ll try this format every week from now on."

Mark nodded. "I’m definitely on board with that."

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why was the marketing meeting turning into a disaster?
2. What two roles did David and Lisa take on to fix the meeting?
3. What were the action items at the end of the meeting?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, the team was wasting time arguing about the logo and started to... (go off-topic).
David stepped in as a... (facilitator) to bring the meeting back to the agenda.
He asked the team to reach a... (consensus) or table the discussion.
Lisa acted as a... (timekeeper) to ensure they finished on time.
They quickly reviewed the budget and David asked for the... (action items).
The team left with clear tasks and a deadline for each... (action item).
David realized that effective meetings require sticking to the agenda and... (wrapping up) on time.

Тема: № 74 Делегирование задач и управление командой (Task Delegation and Team Management)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. to delegate [ˈdelɪɡeɪt] — делегировать, поручать задачи
2. to micromanage [ˈmaɪkrəʊmænɪdʒ] — мелочно контролировать, управлять каждым шагом
3. to empower [ɪmˈpaʊə] — давать полномочия, вдохновлять
4. capacity [kəˈpæsəti] — способность, загрузка, возможности
5. accountability [əˌkaʊntəˈbɪləti] — ответственность, подотчетность
6. to trust [trʌst] — доверять

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~440 слов.
"Alex, the client presentation is due tomorrow, and the slides are still full of errors," said Sarah, the department director, looking at Alex’s desk. 

Alex looked exhausted. Dark circles were under his eyes. "I know, Sarah. I’m trying to fix them, but I also had to rewrite the report and answer client emails. I just don’t have the capacity to do everything myself."

Sarah pulled up a chair. "Alex, you were promoted to team lead because you are an excellent specialist. But you are not acting like a manager. You are trying to do everyone’s job."

"But if I don’t check their work, they make mistakes!" Alex protested. 

"That’s because you micromanage them," Sarah explained gently. "You don’t give them clear instructions, and then you rewrite everything at the last minute. They never learn, and you burn out. You need to learn how to delegate."

"How? Last time I delegated, the result was a mess," Alex sighed.

"Delegation is not just dumping work on someone," Sarah said. "It’s about matching the task to the person’s capacity, giving clear expectations, and then trusting them to do it. You must empower your team to take accountability for their own work."

Alex thought about this. "So, I should have told Mark exactly what the slides needed, given him a deadline, and let him do it?"

"Exactly," Sarah nodded. "And if he makes a mistake, you review it together and give constructive feedback. That’s how people grow. If you do everything yourself, you are not a leader; you are a bottleneck."

The next day, Alex tried a new approach. He called his team for a quick meeting. "Okay, for the new project, Mark, you are in charge of the data analysis. Lisa, you handle the slide design. I will review the final draft on Thursday. Do you have any questions?"

Mark looked surprised. "Really? I can lead the data part?"

"Yes, I trust you," Alex said firmly. "Let me know if you need help, but the ownership is yours."

On Thursday, Mark’s analysis was excellent, and Lisa’s slides were beautiful. Alex only made a few minor suggestions. 

"Great job, everyone," Alex said, feeling a huge weight lift off his shoulders. "This is exactly how we should work."

He realized that true management wasn’t about being the hardest worker in the room. It was about empowering others, trusting the team, and letting go of the need to control every single detail.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why was Alex exhausted and making mistakes?
2. What is the difference between delegating and "dumping work" according to Sarah?
3. How did the team react when Alex gave them ownership of their tasks?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Alex was exhausted because he tried to do everything himself and lacked... (capacity).
Sarah told him he was starting to... (micromanage) instead of leading.
She explained that good management requires learning how to... (delegate) effectively.
Delegation means giving clear expectations and then learning to... (trust) the team.
Alex must... (empower) his employees to take... (accountability) for their work.
The next day, Alex assigned specific tasks to Mark and Lisa and let them take... (ownership).
The result was excellent, and Alex realized that a leader should not be a... (bottleneck).

Тема: № 75 Обратная связь 360 градусов и развитие сотрудников (360-Degree Feedback and Employee Development)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. 360-degree feedback [ˌθriː hʌndrəd ənd ˈsɪksti dɪˈɡriː ˈfiːdbæk] — всесторонняя оценка (от коллег, подчиненных и руководителей)
2. blind spot [blaɪnd spɒt] — слепая зона (неосознаваемая проблема)
3. peer review [pɪə rɪˈvjuː] — оценка коллег
4. constructive [kənˈstrʌktɪv] — конструктивный
5. self-awareness [ˌself əˈweənəs] — самосознание, самоанализ
6. to implement [ˈɪmplɪmənt] — внедрять, применять

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~450 слов.
"Tom, it’s time for your annual review," said Elena, the HR manager. "This year, we are implementing a new 360-degree feedback system. You will receive anonymous feedback not just from me, but from your peers and the junior staff you mentor."

Tom smiled confidently. "No problem. I work hard, and I always meet my deadlines. I’m sure it will be great."

A week later, Tom sat in Elena’s office to review the results. His manager’s feedback was positive, but the peer reviews contained a surprising comment. 

"Let’s look at this," Elena said, pointing to the screen. "Three of your colleagues mentioned that while your technical work is excellent, you often interrupt others in meetings and dismiss their ideas without discussion."

Tom frowned, feeling defensive. "That’s not true. I’m just efficient. I don’t have time for long, pointless discussions."

"Tom, this is what we call a blind spot," Elena said gently. "You don’t see it, but your behavior makes the team feel undervalued. This peer review is not an attack; it’s constructive feedback to help you grow. High self-awareness is a key trait of a senior leader."

Tom was quiet for a moment. He thought about the last few meetings. He remembered cutting off Sarah mid-sentence and rolling his eyes when Mark suggested a new tool. A wave of realization hit him. 

"I... I didn’t realize I was doing that," Tom admitted, his voice softer. "I thought I was just keeping things moving."

"Intentions matter, but impact matters more," Elena replied. "The good news is, this is completely fixable. What can you implement to improve this?"

Tom took a deep breath. "I can start by actively listening. I will make a rule for myself: I won’t speak until the other person has completely finished their thought. And I will ask for their opinion before giving mine."

"That’s a fantastic action plan," Elena smiled. "Let’s check in on this in three months. I believe in your potential, Tom."

Over the next few weeks, Tom consciously practiced active listening. It was hard at first, but he noticed a change. His colleagues started sharing more ideas, and the team’s atmosphere became much more collaborative. 

At the next team lunch, Sarah turned to him. "Tom, I just wanted to say, I really appreciate how you listened to my proposal yesterday. It felt great."

Tom smiled genuinely. "Thank you, Sarah. I’m still learning, but I value your input."

He realized that 360-degree feedback wasn’t about finding faults. It was a powerful tool for uncovering blind spots and fostering genuine professional development.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What was the new feedback system introduced by HR?
2. What was the main criticism Tom received from his peers?
3. What action plan did Tom create to improve his behavior?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, HR introduced a... (360-degree feedback) system, including peer reviews.
Tom was confident, but the feedback revealed a... (blind spot): he often interrupted others.
Elena explained that this was... (constructive) feedback to help him grow.
Tom realized he lacked... (self-awareness) about how his behavior impacted the team.
He decided to... (implement) a rule to actively listen and wait for others to finish speaking.
Over time, the team atmosphere became more... (collaborative).
Tom learned that feedback is a powerful tool for uncovering blind spots and... (development).

Тема: № 76 Онбординг новых сотрудников (Onboarding New Employees)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. onboarding [ˈɒnbɔːdɪŋ] — процесс адаптации нового сотрудника
2. orientation [ˌɔːriənˈteɪʃn] — вводный инструктаж, ориентация
3. mentor [ˈmentɔː] — наставник, куратор
4. to integrate [ˈɪntɪɡreɪt] — интегрировать, вливаться в коллектив
5. employee handbook [ɪmˈplɔɪiː ˈhændbʊk] — справочник сотрудника
6. to settle in [ˈsetl ɪn] — обжиться, освоиться

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~420 слов.
It was Julia’s first day at her new marketing job. She walked into the office feeling a mix of excitement and nervousness. 

"Good morning, Julia! Welcome to the team," said David, the HR manager, greeting her with a warm smile. "I’m so glad you’re here."

"Thank you, David. I’m ready to start," Julia replied.

"Great. First, let’s go to the conference room for a quick orientation," David said. "I’ll walk you through the company culture, the employee handbook, and show you where the coffee machine is—arguably the most important part of the day!"

Julia laughed, feeling her nerves ease a bit. After the orientation, David didn’t just leave her alone at a desk with a pile of paperwork. 

"Julia, I’d like you to meet Mark," David said, leading her to a desk. "Mark is a senior designer, and he will be your mentor for the first month. He’s here to answer any questions, no matter how small."

"Hi, Julia!" Mark said, standing up and shaking her hand. "Don’t worry about learning everything on day one. My job is to help you integrate into the team smoothly. Let’s start with a quick tour of the office, and then we can grab lunch together so you can meet the rest of the crew."

"That sounds amazing, thank you," Julia said, genuinely relieved. 

Over the next few weeks, Mark checked in with her daily. He explained the team’s workflow, introduced her to key software, and made sure she felt included in team chats and meetings. When Julia made a small mistake with a client email, Mark didn’t scold her. Instead, he sat with her, explained the company’s tone of voice, and helped her rewrite it.

By the end of her first month, Julia didn’t feel like the "new girl" anymore. She knew everyone’s names, understood the company’s goals, and had already contributed to two successful campaigns. 

During her one-month check-in, David asked, "How are you settling in, Julia?"

"Perfectly," Julia smiled. "The onboarding process was incredible. Having a mentor like Mark made all the difference. I feel like I truly belong here."

David nodded. "That’s exactly what we aim for. A good onboarding process isn’t just about filling out forms. It’s about making people feel welcome, supported, and ready to do their best work from day one."

Julia realized that a company’s culture is defined not just by its mission statement, but by how it treats people when they first walk through the door.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)

2. What role did Mark play in Julia’s first month?
3. Why was Julia’s onboarding experience successful?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):

Mark’s goal was to help Julia... (integrate) into the team smoothly.
He gave her a tour, took her to... (lunch), and helped her with mistakes.
By the end of the month, Julia felt she had managed to... (settle in) perfectly.
She realized that good... (onboarding) is about making new employees feel welcome and supported.

Тема: № 77 Работа с трудными коллегами и токсичным поведением (Dealing with Difficult Colleagues and Toxic Behavior)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. toxic behavior [ˈtɒksɪk bɪˈheɪvjə] — токсичное поведение
2. passive-aggressive [ˌpæsɪv əˈɡresɪv] — пассивно-агрессивный
3. to address [əˈdres] — решать проблему, открыто говорить о ней
4. to set boundaries [set ˈbaʊndəriz] — устанавливать границы
5. to document [ˈdɒkjument] — документировать, фиксировать
6. Human Resources (HR) [ˌhjuːmən rɪˈzɔːsɪz] — отдел кадров

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~440 слов.
Emma loved her job as a content writer, but there was one major problem: her colleague, Greg. 

During team meetings, Greg would often make passive-aggressive comments about her work. "Oh, Emma wrote this? Well, it’s certainly... creative," he would say with a smirk, making the whole room uncomfortable. He also had a habit of taking credit for her ideas in emails to the manager.

At first, Emma tried to ignore it, hoping he would stop. But his toxic behavior only escalated. She felt stressed and dreaded going to work. 

One afternoon, she vented to her friend and senior colleague, Lisa. "I don’t know what to do, Lisa. Greg is making my life miserable, but I don’t want to cause drama."

Lisa listened carefully. "Emma, ignoring toxic behavior doesn’t make it go away; it enables it. You need to address it directly and set boundaries."

"But what if he gets angry or denies it?" Emma asked nervously.

"Stay calm and stick to the facts," Lisa advised. "Don’t attack his character. Just describe the specific behavior and how it affects your work. And most importantly, start to document everything. Save the emails where he takes credit, and write down the dates and times of his comments."

Emma took Lisa’s advice. She started keeping a private log of Greg’s behavior. A week later, during a project meeting, Greg did it again. "Emma’s draft is okay, but I had to completely rewrite the strategy to make it usable," he announced.

Emma took a deep breath. She didn’t yell. She looked at him calmly and said, "Greg, I’m surprised to hear that, because the strategy document has my name on it, and I didn’t receive any requests for a rewrite from you. If you have specific feedback, I’m happy to discuss it directly after the meeting."

The room went silent. Greg looked taken aback. "Uh, well, maybe I misunderstood," he mumbled.

After the meeting, Emma didn’t stop there. She compiled her documented evidence and requested a private meeting with the HR manager. She presented the facts objectively, without emotional accusations. 

"Thank you for bringing this to our attention, Emma," the HR manager said seriously. "We take toxic behavior very seriously. We will speak with Greg and monitor the situation."

Within a few weeks, Greg’s behavior changed dramatically. He became much more professional and stopped making snide remarks. 

Emma felt a huge sense of relief. She realized that dealing with difficult colleagues isn’t about starting a fight. It’s about protecting your peace, setting clear boundaries, and using professional channels to address the problem.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What kind of toxic behavior did Greg exhibit towards Emma?
2. What two pieces of advice did Lisa give Emma?
3. How did Emma respond to Greg’s comment during the meeting?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Emma’s colleague Greg made... (passive-aggressive) comments and took credit for her work.
Emma felt stressed, so she asked her friend Lisa for... (advice).
Lisa told her to stop ignoring it and start to... (document) everything.
During a meeting, Emma calmly... (addressed) the issue by stating the facts.
Greg was taken aback and... (mumbled) an excuse.
Emma then took her documented evidence to... (Human Resources / HR).
HR took the... (toxic behavior) seriously and spoke with Greg.
Emma learned that dealing with difficult people requires setting... (boundaries) and staying professional.

Тема: № 78 Тайм-менеджмент в условиях многозадачности (Time Management in Multitasking Conditions)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. multitasking [ˈmʌltitɑːskɪŋ] — многозадачность
2. cognitive load [ˈkɒɡnətɪv ləʊd] — когнитивная нагрузка
3. to switch tasks [swɪtʃ tɑːsks] — переключаться между задачами
4. to batch [bætʃ] — группировать, объединять в пакеты
5. priority matrix [praɪˈɒrəti ˈmeɪtrɪks] — матрица приоритетов
6. to focus [ˈfəʊkəs] — сосредотачиваться

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~450 слов.
"James, you sent the wrong file to the client," said his manager, Anna, looking at her screen with a frown. 

James panicked. "What? No, I attached the correct one! Let me check." He opened his email and his heart sank. He had attached an internal draft instead of the final version. "I’m so sorry, Anna. I don’t know how that happened."

Anna sighed. "James, I’ve noticed this happening a lot lately. You’re trying to answer emails, write reports, and take phone calls all at the same time. Multitasking is destroying your focus."

"But I have so much to do!" James argued. "If I don’t switch tasks constantly, I’ll fall behind."

"Actually, science says the opposite," Anna explained. "Every time you switch tasks, your brain experiences a high cognitive load. It takes time to refocus, which leads to mistakes, like sending the wrong file. You aren’t saving time; you’re losing it."

James looked defeated. "So, what should I do? I can’t just ignore my emails all day."

"You don’t have to ignore them. You need to batch them," Anna said. "Instead of checking your inbox every five minutes, schedule two or three specific times a day to process emails. For example, 9:00 AM, 1:00 PM, and 4:30 PM."

"That makes sense," James said, taking notes. "But what about my projects?"

"Use a priority matrix," Anna suggested. "Divide your tasks into four boxes: urgent and important, important but not urgent, urgent but not important, and neither. Focus only on the top box first. Close all other tabs and notifications while you work on it."

James decided to try this new system the very next day. He turned off his email notifications. He put his phone in his drawer. He looked at his priority matrix and chose one major report to work on for the next 90 minutes. 

It was surprisingly difficult at first. His brain kept urging him to check his phone. But he resisted. When the 90 minutes were up, he had finished a section of the report that usually took him half a day. 

At 1:00 PM, he opened his email and cleared his inbox in 20 minutes because he wasn’t distracted by anything else.

At the end of the week, Anna stopped by his desk. "I haven’t seen any mistakes from you this week, James. And you look much less stressed."

James smiled. "I feel great. I stopped multitasking, started to batch my work, and my focus has improved dramatically. I’m actually getting things done."

He realized that true productivity isn’t about doing a hundred things at once. It’s about giving one thing your full attention at a time.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. What mistake did James make because of multitasking?
2. Why does Anna say that switching tasks is bad for the brain?
3. What two techniques did Anna suggest to help James manage his time?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, James sent the wrong file to a client because he was... (multitasking).
Anna explained that constantly trying to... (switch tasks) increases... (cognitive load) and causes mistakes.
She advised him to... (batch) his emails instead of checking them constantly.
She also suggested using a... (priority matrix) to decide what to do first.
The next day, James turned off notifications to improve his... (focus).
He worked on one major report for 90 minutes without interruptions.
By the end of the week, James made no mistakes and felt much less... (stressed).
He learned that productivity is about giving one thing your full... (attention).

Тема: № 79 Приоритизация задач (матрица Эйзенхауэра и другие методы) (Task Prioritization)
Уровень: B1 (Pre-Intermediate)

1. Vocabulary (Словарь перед чтением)
Здесь всего 6 выражений. Они помогут понять текст, но не перегрузят память.
1. urgent [ˈɜːdʒənt] — срочный
2. important [ɪmˈpɔːtənt] — важный
3. to delegate [ˈdelɪɡeɪt] — делегировать, поручать
4. to postpone [pəˈspəʊn] — откладывать
5. Eisenhower Matrix [aɪzənˈhaʊə ˈmeɪtrɪks] — матрица Эйзенхауэра
6. overwhelmed [ˌəʊvəˈwelmd] — перегруженный, ошеломленный

2. The Story (История)
Текст с живыми диалогами. Объем: ~430 слов.
Sarah stared at her computer screen, feeling completely overwhelmed. Her to-do list had twenty items, and she didn't know where to start. She was answering emails, writing a report, and trying to plan a team event all at the same time.

"Sarah, you look like you're about to crash," said Mark, her manager, stopping by her desk. "What's going on?"

"I have too much to do, and everything feels like an emergency," Sarah admitted, rubbing her temples. "I don't know how to prioritize."

Mark pulled up a chair. "Let me show you a simple tool. It’s called the Eisenhower Matrix. It helps you divide tasks into four categories based on two questions: Is it urgent? Is it important?"

Sarah opened a blank document. "Okay, explain the four categories."

"Quadrant one: Urgent and Important," Mark said. "These are crises or deadlines. Do them immediately. For example, the client report due today."

"Got it," Sarah said, typing it down. "What about answering every single email?"

"That’s Quadrant three: Urgent but Not Important," Mark explained. "These are interruptions. They feel urgent, but they don't contribute to your long-term goals. You should delegate them or batch them for later."

"And what about planning the team event?" Sarah asked.

"Quadrant two: Important but Not Urgent," Mark smiled. "This is where real progress happens. Strategic planning, learning new skills. You must schedule time for these, or they will become urgent later."

"Finally, Quadrant four: Not Urgent and Not Important," Mark added. "Like scrolling social media or organizing your desktop icons. Eliminate or postpone these."

Sarah looked at her list. "Wow. I’ve been spending 80% of my time in Quadrant three and four."

"Exactly," Mark said. "So, what’s your first action?"

"I will finish the client report now," Sarah said confidently. "Then, I will ask Tom to handle the event catering, and I will schedule two hours tomorrow for strategic planning."

"Perfect," Mark nodded. "Prioritization isn't about doing more things. It’s about doing the right things."

By the end of the week, Sarah felt a massive difference. She was no longer putting out fires all day. She realized that using a simple framework like the Eisenhower Matrix gave her control over her time and reduced her stress significantly.

3. Retelling Practice (Практика пересказа)
Шаг 1. Ответь на простые вопросы (устно или письменно):
1. Why did Sarah feel overwhelmed at the beginning of the story?
2. What are the two questions used in the Eisenhower Matrix?
3. What did Sarah decide to do with the team event planning?

Шаг 2. Пересказ по плану (Fill in the blanks / Соедини идеи):
First, Sarah had a long to-do list and felt completely... (overwhelmed).
Mark introduced her to the... (Eisenhower Matrix) to help her prioritize.
Tasks that are urgent and important must be done... (immediately).
Answering every email is urgent but not important, so she should... (delegate) or batch it.
Strategic planning is important but not urgent, so she must... (schedule) time for it.
Sarah realized she was wasting time on tasks that were neither... (urgent) nor important.
She decided to finish the client report first and ask Tom to help with the... (event).
She learned that prioritization is about doing the... (right) things, not more things.
